> AGENT

Thanks — that's a clean, encouraging result, and you're right to flag it as suggestive rather than definitive. A few thoughts:

**The two differences are confounded.** You changed *baud* (921600 → 115200) and *stream count* (ASCII+binary → ASCII-only) at the same time, so these 4 runs can't tell us which one bought the reliability. Last night's trouble was specifically "ascii and bin at the same time" at 921600 — so the most likely culprits are either the higher data rate stressing the cold-open buffer, or the binary-stream config itself (the reg-75 serial-port-on-TTL stuff we fixed earlier). This run exercises neither.

**Run 1 is the meaningful one for the zero-parse bug.** The intermittent `ASCII: none / Binary: none` is cold-open sensitive — it bites the *first* open after power-up. You powered fresh and your 14:50:12 run was the genuine cold open, and it parsed fine. Runs 2–4 are warm and matter less. So: one clean cold open at 115200 ASCII-only. Good data point, but n=1 for the case that actually matters.

**The numbers check out.** 39.5–39.8 Hz VNYMR, ~122 B/msg, 49 kbit/s = 42% of the link. That's the configured 40 Hz ASCII with comfortable headroom — no back-pressure, which is consistent with no parse trouble.

If you want to actually pin down what fixed reliability, vary one knob at a time, each from a cold power-cycle (since cold-open is the sensitive moment):

- **Hold baud, add binary back** (115200, ASCII+bin) — tests whether "both streams" is the trigger independent of baud.
- **Hold stream-count, raise baud** (921600, ASCII-only) — tests whether 921600 alone destabilizes the cold open.

Whichever reproduces the zero-parse points at the real variable, and that's the one to instrument with the env-gated raw dump (Todo #1).

What's the goal this morning — chase down the zero-parse, or get both streams reliable at 115200 first since that's the proven path toward the fc.py 200 Hz work?

> DEVELOPER

I agree, but this was simply the state on power on, so it's reasonble to test. One other thing I think we might want to do, but probably after we reproduce the problem, is add a mode where we collect the data during a run so that we can actually capture the bad data. I'm thinking we capture it in memory not try to stream it to a file in real time so as to minimize changes. It fails right away so I'm hoping/assuming we have enough RAM to run a few minutes. Again, I'm thinking we reporduce the issue first, which means enabling binary as the second stream. Thoughts

> AGENT

Both ideas are sound, and they line up well with the existing plan. Thoughts:

**In-memory capture — RAM is a non-issue, and in-memory is the *right* call for a subtler reason than just "fewer changes."** Streaming to a file mid-loop adds write syscalls inside the read path, which perturbs the very timing we're trying to characterize — it could mask or shift the bug. A growing `Vec<u8>` (dump once at exit) keeps the hot loop clean. On the numbers: 115200 maxes at ~11.5 kB/s, so 5 minutes is ~3.5 MB; even at 921600 fully saturated it's ~90 kB/s → ~27 MB for 5 min. Your RPi5 has GBs — you could capture an hour and not notice. So drop any worry about RAM; capture liberally.

Worth naming explicitly: **this capture mode *is* Todo #1's "env-gated raw dump" [[8]]**, just realized as collect-in-RAM-then-dump rather than live-stream. Same artifact (a raw byte log you can diff against `test-data/both-streams.bin`), better mechanism. So building it isn't a detour — it's the instrument the bug-hunt already called for. I'd gate it on an env var (e.g. `RW_VN100_CAPTURE=path`) so the normal path is byte-for-byte unchanged. And since the bug bites at cold open, timestamping the chunks (`Instant` per read) would let us see exactly *when* in the first second things go wrong.

**Reproduction by enabling binary — agree it's the next step, but two cautions so we reproduce the *right* failure:**

1. **Don't overflow the link, or you'll get a saturation drop masquerading as the cold-open bug.** At 115200 you're already at 42% with 40 Hz ASCII. If binary goes on at a high rate (and the 200 Hz fc.py goal tempts that), ASCII+binary can exceed 115.2 kbit/s — that's a *bandwidth* failure, a different beast from the intermittent cold-open zero-parse. To target the cold-open bug specifically, pick binary rates that comfortably fit under the link; to reproduce last night's high-baud condition, go to 921600 where both streams have headroom.

2. **Enabling binary right now means a port reopen.** The single-connection step (`0.3.0-6`) isn't built yet, so it's `set-bin …` (reg 75) as one command, then `bench` as another — and each reopen is itself a wedge die-roll per the notes. That's fine for reproducing, but keep it in mind: if the failure shows up *between* the two commands, it may be the reopen, not the stream config.

So my suggested order: from the current clean 115200 state, add binary at a rate that fits the link and re-run the cold-open test (one knob changed). If it reproduces — baud wasn't needed, and we instrument right there. If it doesn't, go to 921600 with both streams to match last night. Want me to work out concrete `set-bin`/`set-bin-hz` values that keep us under 115.2 kbit/s before you try it?

> AGENT

Got the numbers I need. The binary frame is `payload + 6` bytes on the wire (sync 1 + groups 1 + mask 2 + CRC 2 = 6 of overhead), and the tool counts wire throughput at 10 bits/byte (8N1), matching your `4850 B/s = 49 kbit/s`. Field payload sizes: time 8, ypr 12, quat 16, gyro 12, accel 12, imu 24, magpres 20.

**Budget.** Link = 11,520 B/s. ASCII at 40 Hz eats ~4,855 B/s (49 kbit/s, 42%), leaving ~6,665 B/s. I'll keep total ≤ ~90% to leave UART jitter headroom (no flow control), so the binary budget is ~5,500 B/s.

**`set-bin=time,accel,gyro`** (payload 32 → 38 B/frame), with ASCII still at 40 Hz:

| Binary Hz | divisor | binary B/s | total B/s | % of 115.2 | verdict |
|----------|---------|-----------|-----------|-----------|---------|
| 50  | 16 | 1,900 | 6,755 | 59% | comfortable |
| **100** | **8** | **3,800** | **8,655** | **75%** | **recommended** |
| 200 | 4 | 7,600 | 12,455 | 108% | ❌ overflows |
| 400 | 2 | 15,200 | — | — | ❌ overflows |

200 Hz is off the table at 115200 with a 38 B frame — that would be a *saturation* drop, exactly the false positive we want to avoid conflating with the cold-open bug.

**Recommended sequence** (each a separate invocation, since the single-connection `-6` step isn't built yet):

```
rw-vn100 set-bin=time,accel,gyro --baud 115200
rw-vn100 set-bin-hz=100 --baud 115200
rw-vn100 bench --baud 115200 2
```

Order matters: `set-bin` first enables the mask (a never-configured output defaults to divisor 20 / 40 Hz and warns), then `set-bin-hz=100` overrides the rate. Expected `bench`: ASCII ~40 Hz + Binary ~100 Hz, ~75% link — a genuine dual stream with clear headroom, so any `ASCII: none / Binary: none` is the real cold-open bug, not back-pressure.

Two notes:
- `set-*` **writes flash**, so this config persists across power cycles. To clear binary later: `rw-vn100 set-bin=off`.
- If you'd rather push toward the fc.py 200 Hz rate while reproducing, you *can* hit 200 Hz binary at 115200 only with a lean frame — e.g. `set-bin=ypr` (18 B) @ 200 Hz = 3,600 B/s → total 73%, or `set-bin=time,gyro` (26 B) @ 200 Hz = 5,200 B/s → total 87% (tight; drop ASCII to 20 Hz via `set-ascii-hz=20` to relax it). But for a clean first reproduction I'd stick with the 100 Hz `time,accel,gyro` row.

Want to run the recommended sequence, or do you want to drive it from the device side yourself and just report back?

> DEVELOPER

I don't think set-* writes flash as we did set's last night and they did not survive the power cycle, please check the code.

> AGENT

You're right, and I was wrong. The code shows the opposite of what I claimed:

- **`Command::SetBin`** (lib.rs:254) and **`Command::SetBinHz`** (lib.rs:285) only call `save_to_flash(...)` **`if persist`** — i.e. only when you pass `--persist`.
- The bare `VNWRG,75,...` write goes to the **volatile register**. VN-100 registers are RAM-backed and revert on power cycle unless explicitly committed to flash (a separate WNV/`VNWNV` command, which is what `save_to_flash` issues).

So without `--persist`, `set-bin` / `set-bin-hz` are exactly volatile — which matches what you saw last night: the configs didn't survive the power cycle. My "set-* writes flash" claim was wrong.

The source of my error: the **`--help` text itself says it writes flash**. From cli.rs:171:

```
"  `set-*` writes flash. Bare `set-bin` re-enables the current mask.\n"
```

That line is misleading — it states an unconditional flash write, but the code only writes flash on `--persist`. So there's a real **doc/behavior mismatch in the help text** worth fixing (the help should say something like "`set-*` writes the volatile register; add `--persist` to also save to flash").

Net effect on the reproduction plan: **good news, actually** — the recommended sequence

```
rw-vn100 set-bin=time,accel,gyro --baud 115200
rw-vn100 set-bin-hz=100 --baud 115200
rw-vn100 bench --baud 115200 2
```

leaves flash untouched, so a power cycle returns the device to ASCII-only. No `set-bin=off` cleanup needed — just power-cycle. And it means each cold-open test starts from the same flash-default state, which is exactly what we want for the cold-open bug hunt.

Want me to note that help-text mismatch as a Todo/bug while we're here, or leave it until after the reproduction work?

> DEVELOPER

note that help-text mismatch as a bug

1	# Bugs
2	
3	This file uses [Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form). It lists known
4	defects we're aware of but haven't scheduled a fix for. Each entry
5	describes what goes wrong, when, and the cost of the failure.
6	
7	Each bug is a `### Issue #N — <title>` subsection. The Issue # is a
8	permanent, monotonic id — assigned once from the allocator below,
9	never reused or renumbered — so the heading doubles as a stable
10	anchor (`#issue-N-…`) that todo / chores / commits can link to. The
11	title may be reworded; the `issue-N` prefix of the anchor stays put.
12	
13	Next Issue #: 3
14	
15	## Bugs
16	
17	### Issue #1 — high-baud reconnect can wedge the VN-100 UART
18	
19	A cross-process reconnect (a fresh `rw-vn100` invocation opening
20	the port) at a high baud can wedge the VN-100's UART completely:
21	afterward the device is silent at *every* baud, recoverable only
22	by a power cycle. The failure is intermittent and probabilistic
23	per reconnect — not a clean threshold — and the odds rise with
24	baud.
25	
26	The locus is narrow. The **in-session** baud switch (the `baud`
27	command sends `VNWRG,05` and talks at the new rate on the live
28	connection) was clean at every baud tested. Only the
29	**fresh-process reopen** at the new baud wedges.
30	
31	- First observation, 2026-06-26 (chained, *no* power cycle
32	  between switches):
33	    - `baud 57600` → verified; `bench --baud 57600` (fresh
34	      process) → clean, 40.0 Hz.
35	    - `--baud 57600 baud 230400` → verified (1 retry); `bench
36	      --baud 230400` (fresh process) → **clean, 40.0 Hz** once.
37	    - The very next fresh-process open at 230400 → no reply;
38	      `--baud 115200 get-hz` → no reply. Silent at both. Power
39	      cycle restored 115200 / 40 Hz.
40	- Controlled retest, 2026-06-26 (**fresh power cycle before each**
41	  baud, explicit `--baud`):
42	    - 57600 → switch verified (1 retry); fresh-process bench
43	      clean, 40.3 Hz.
44	    - 115200 (boots here) → fresh-process bench clean, 39.8 Hz.
45	    - 230400 → switch verified; fresh-process bench clean,
46	      40.0 Hz.
47	    - 921600 → switch verified; fresh-process bench → **wedged on
48	      the first reconnect** (no reply). Power cycle restored
49	      115200 / 40 Hz.
50	- All baud changes were volatile (no `--persist`), so every power
51	  cycle reverts to the flash default 115200 / 40 Hz.
52	- 115200 has a clean record across many reconnects. 57600 and
53	  230400 each survived one clean reconnect from a fresh power
54	  cycle — but 230400 wedged in the chained run, so a clean device
55	  state helps and is not a guarantee. 921600 wedged immediately.
56	  None of the single clean runs is a reliability proof.
57	- The bench link is RS-232: a Gearmo GM-FTD12-LED-C USB-C to
58	  RS-232 adapter (±5.7 V output, rated 300 bps–460 Kbps) into the
59	  VN-100's RS-232 port. 921600 is ~2× that rating. We think the
60	  cause is RS-232 slew-rate limiting — on the adapter's
61	  transceiver and on the VN-100's own onboard RS-232 transceiver,
62	  neither swappable — mis-framing bytes into the device at high
63	  baud, which its firmware reacts to by wedging. It is *not* an
64	  FTDI control-line transient: no handshake lines are wired. Full
65	  analysis: chores-01 [[1]].
66	- This is an RS-232-rig finding. The RPi5 flight target uses a
67	  direct 3.3 V TTL UART with no RS-232 transceivers in the path,
68	  so the wedge must be retested there before it is treated as a
69	  VN-100 limit. We think it may not reproduce on TTL.
70	
71	**Cost / why it matters:** an intermittent *unrecoverable* failure
72	that passes bench testing is the worst kind for flight use — it
73	ships, then wedges the IMU mid-flight with no recovery but cutting
74	power. "Works sometimes" is not safe. Binary output already gives
75	200 Hz at the rock-solid 115200, so there is no reason to chase a
76	higher device baud.
77	
78	### Issue #2 — `read_reply` closure parameter named `matches` collides with the `matches!` macro
79	
80	In `read_reply` (`src/main.rs`), the predicate closure parameter is
81	named `matches`, while the same function body uses the std `matches!`
82	macro to classify a read error kind. Two unrelated things share the
83	name in one scope:
84	
85	- `matches!(e.kind(), ErrorKind::TimedOut | ErrorKind::WouldBlock)` —
86	  the standard-library macro (note the `!`), a pattern test on the
87	  I/O error.
88	- `matches(&candidate)` — the caller-supplied `FnMut(&str) -> bool`
89	  closure that decides whether an assembled reply line is the one
90	  we're waiting for. It is **not** an exact-equality test: every
91	  call site passes a `starts_with` *prefix* predicate (e.g.
92	  `|l| l.starts_with("$VNWRG,75")`), and `transact` further OR's in
93	  a `$VNERR` prefix so a device-error reply also terminates the read
94	  (`read_reply(port, deadline, |l| accept(l) || l.starts_with("$VNERR"))`).
95	  `read_reply` slices `candidate` from the last `$` before testing,
96	  so the prefix is anchored to the real reply, not stray leading
97	  bytes.
98	
99	The closure threads through `transact_retry -> transact -> read_reply`
100	as `impl Fn(&str) -> bool`. A reader scanning `read_reply` sees the
101	`matches!(...)` macro first and reasonably assumes the later
102	`matches(...)` is the same thing, when the actual closure invocation
103	is the prefix-based line-acceptance test.
104	
105	- Proposed fix: rename the parameter (and its doc-comment mention) to
106	  `is_acceptable` — it is a function parameter and call sites are
107	  unaffected (the closure is passed positionally).
108	- Pure readability defect: behavior is correct; the name collision
109	  costs reader time and invites misreading.
110	
111	**Cost / why it matters:** no runtime impact, but `read_reply` is the
112	core of every command's reply path, so the confusion tax is paid by
113	anyone tracing how `GetHZ` (or any transact) reads a reply.
114	
115	# References
116	
117	[1]: chores/chores-01.md#rs-232-link-analysis
118

> AGENT

Before writing the entry I re-read the full help string in context — and there's actually **no mismatch**. I misread it last turn by quoting line 171 in isolation. The sentence spans two `push_str` lines (cli.rs:170–171), and rendered it reads:

> Output config — each verb owns one register; **`--persist` on a `set-*` writes flash.** Bare `set-bin` re-enables the current mask.

The subject is `--persist`, not `set-*`. So the help text says exactly what the code does: a `set-*` writes flash *only when you add `--persist`*. That matches lib.rs:254 / lib.rs:285 (`if persist { save_to_flash(...) }`).

My earlier quote chopped off the leading "`--persist` on a" because the line break split the sentence, which made it look like an unconditional "set-* writes flash." That was my reading error, not a code/doc bug.

So I'd recommend **not** filing this — there's nothing to fix. The substantive conclusion from before still holds and is the part that matters: without `--persist`, `set-bin`/`set-bin-hz` are volatile and revert on power cycle, which is what you observed last night.

That said — the wrapping clearly tripped *me*, and a human skimming `--help` could hit the same ambiguity, since the rendered output also breaks the line mid-sentence. If you want, I could file a much narrower **readability** nit (reword so `--persist`'s conditional role survives the line wrap, e.g. "`set-*` verbs write the volatile register; add `--persist` to also save to flash") — but that's a wording polish, not a correctness bug. Your call: file the readability nit, or drop it?

> AGENT

Done. The rule now reads:

> **Semicolons**
> A semicolon that splices a detail onto a notion without adding information just fragments the sentence, and the split reads as a boundary that invites a misread. Two surfaces, two remedies:
> - **Inside a bullet** — … break the clauses into indented sub-bullets so the structure shows.
> - **In running prose** (intro paragraphs, doc comments, help text, commit bodies) there is no sub-bullet to demote into. Use a plain sentence break — a period — instead, or restructure when the detail genuinely subordinates.
> Not absolute: a true sentence-join of two closely related independent clauses, or a very short tight pair inside a bullet, can stay joined when breaking would be more noise than signal.

```
1	# Cycle protocol
2	
3	This protocol uses [Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form). It
4	contains instructions on how a commit cycle is accomplished.
5	
6	The artifact a cycle produces is whatever the bot generates from
7	the conversation — code, prose, an image, a song, a screenplay.
8	The steps below use a Rust crate as the running example (the
9	cargo cycle, `Cargo.toml` versioning); substitute your medium's
10	equivalents — this project's manifest is recorded in
11	[versioning.md](versioning.md).
12	
13	## Cycles
14	
15	A cycle has three phases:
16	
17	- **[Preparation](#preparation)** (`X.Y.Z-0`) — the cycle's
18	  first commit, when it needs setup (a lightweight cycle omits
19	  it and starts at `-1` — see
20	  [versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)). Sets up the
21	  cycle:
22	  - Bump the version-of-record to `X.Y.Z-0` (where it lives
23	    and the suffix scheme are project-specific — see
24	    [versioning.md](versioning.md)).
25	  - Pick up a `## Todo` item (typically the top-ranked,
26	    #1) into `## In Progress` (bold title + succinct problem
27	    statement + plan ladder).
28	  - Open the [chores section](#chores-sections).
29	- **[Work-N](#work-n)** (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …) — the
30	  commits that implement the change. As many as the change
31	  needs; each runs through the
32	  [per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow).
33	- **[Close-out](#close-out)** (bare `X.Y.Z`) — the cycle's
34	  last commit. Bookkeeping only:
35	  - Move the picked-up item to a one-line entry in
36	    `## Done`.
37	  - Move the `## In Progress` block into the
38	    [chores section](#chores-sections).
39	  - Optionally update `notes/README.md` if functionality
40	    changed.
41	
42	A cycle's commits are published to the project remote
43	either incrementally or as one batch at close-out; the
44	result must always be published at close-out. See
45	[Pushing](#pushing).
46	
47	**Sub-cycles.** When a Work commit's scope grows enough to
48	warrant its own ladder, it subdivides — `X.Y.Z-3.0`
49	Preparation, `X.Y.Z-3.1` / `X.Y.Z-3.2` Work, `X.Y.Z-3`
50	Close-out. The same three-phase shape applies recursively
51	at every depth. See [Numbering](#numbering) for the
52	suffix rule and [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)
53	for the local-ladder mechanics.
54	
55	## Chores sections
56	
57	A **chores section** is a `##` section in
58	`notes/chores/chores-NN.md` recording landed work. In
59	general, every commit that lands on the permanent branch
60	should have a reference to it on a `Commits:` list in a
61	chores file.
62	
63	The phrase **"Open" the chores section** means append a
64	`##` header to the current `notes/chores/chores-NN.md`
65	with the title it records (e.g. `## refactor: foo bar`),
66	followed by an **empty `Commits:`** line. The narrative
67	body stays empty until close-out (see [Close-out](#close-out));
68	the `Commits:` line is backfilled later, once the commit is
69	permanent (see [Commits backfill](#commits-backfill) below).
70	
71	Fuller chores conventions (content rules, header sync,
72	design subsection pattern, `Commits:` formatting) live in
73	AGENTS.md [Chores conventions](../AGENTS.md#chores-conventions).
74	
75	### Commits backfill
76	
77	A chores section's `Commits:` line cites the commit(s) it
78	records, by SHA — but a SHA isn't stable until the commit
79	lands on a **permanent branch** (`main`, or a long-lived
80	release/patch branch that won't be rewritten); a rebase or
81	squash rewrites it on the way. So:
82	
83	- A chores section is **opened with an empty `Commits:`** line.
84	- **Backfill once the commit is on a permanent branch**,
85	  where its SHA is final. A commit can't record its own SHA
86	  (that would change the hash), so the fill always lands one
87	  push later: **each push backfills the `Commits:` of the
88	  commits the previous push made permanent.** On a topic
89	  branch the sections instead wait until the branch lands —
90	  so no SHA is ever written that a later rebase could
91	  invalidate.
92	
93	Use `[[N]]` refs — several as `[[N]],[[M]]` only when one
94	section records multiple commits (a merge non-ff close-out) —
95	with the commit URL + 40-hex SHA in the file's `# References`
96	(format in AGENTS.md
97	[Chores commit references](../AGENTS.md#chores-commit-references)).
98	A section's `##` title matches its commit title, so a rare
99	deliberate rewrite of a permanent-branch commit re-syncs via
100	`git log --grep "<title>"`.
101	
102	The per-push cadence is a project choice, not dogma — a
103	**per-close-out** model (recording a cycle's SHAs at its
104	close-out) is equally valid. The one invariant: a recorded
105	SHA must be permanent.
106	
107	## Preparation
108	
109	The cycle's first commit (`X.Y.Z-0`), when the cycle needs
110	setup (a lightweight cycle omits it — see
111	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)):
112	
113	- **Bump the version-of-record** to `X.Y.Z-0`. Where it
114	  lives, the suffix scheme, and any derived files (a
115	  lockfile, a sourced manifest version) are
116	  project-specific — see [versioning.md](versioning.md).
117	- **Move a `## Todo` item** (if the cycle has one) into
118	  `## In Progress` and the todo item should have:
119	  - A **bold title line** — that will be the chores
120	    section header, minus the `## ` prefix.
121	  - A **succinct problem statement**; add if one is needed
122	  - A **plan ladder**.
123	- **Open the [chores section](#chores-sections)** —
124	  append a `##` header with the title it records — the
125	  cycle's anticipated close-out title.
126	
127	## Work-N
128	
129	The cycle's work commits (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …)
130	implement the change. As many as needed:
131	
132	- Each commit runs through the
133	  **[per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow)**.
134	- **Interim pushes** are optional (backup, progress
135	  visibility).
136	- Close-out is the only mandatory push (see
137	  [Pushing](#pushing)).
138	- **Subdivide into a sub-cycle** if a Work commit's
139	  scope grows enough (see
140	  [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)).
141	
142	## Close-out
143	
144	The cycle's last commit (bare `X.Y.Z`) does bookkeeping
145	only — the commit body describes that bookkeeping, not
146	what happens post-squash:
147	
148	- **Move the picked-up item** from `## In Progress` to a
149	  one-line entry (with a chores `[N]` ref) in `## Done`.
150	- **Move the `## In Progress` block into the
151	  [chores section](#chores-sections)** (the title-only
152	  placeholder opened during Preparation):
153	  - Cut the problem statement + plan ladder out of
154	    `## In Progress` (drop the bold title line — the
155	    chores `##` header already carries that string).
156	  - Paste under the chores `##` header.
157	  - Sync the chores header to the **final** commit title
158	    if the cycle's scope shifted; update every anchor
159	    back-reference.
160	  - Add an `### As-built ladder` listing the cycle's
161	    commits; optional `### Outcome` notes.
162	  - Replace `## In Progress` with
163	    `_No cycle currently in progress._`.
164	- **Update `notes/README.md`** if functionality changed
165	  (new flags, new subcommands, changed behavior).
166	
167	Whether to **squash** the cycle into one commit before the
168	publishing push, or push as-is, is decided at push time —
169	see [Pushing](#pushing).
170	
171	## Numbering
172	
173	Each commit's phase is encoded in the version suffix — `-0`
174	Preparation, `-1`/`-2`/… Work, bare `X.Y.Z` Close-out,
175	recursively for sub-cycles. The full scheme — disambiguation,
176	nesting, optional Preparation, the project's version-of-record
177	format, and the per-phase bump — lives in
178	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering), which is the
179	single source of truth for this repo's versioning.
180	
181	## Per-commit flow
182	
183	Every commit (Preparation, each Work commit, Close-out) goes
184	through:
185	
186	1. **Mark this commit `(current)`** as the first edit in
187	   `notes/todo.md > ## In Progress`.
188	2. **Do the work** (see [Iterative work](#iterative-work)
189	   for the loop-and-squash technique).
190	3. **Flip this commit `(current)` → `(done)`** in `## In
191	   Progress` — before the cargo cycle and the commit.
192	4. **Validate the artifact** — a medium-specific step, skip-able
193	   for notes-only commits, mandatory at close-out. For the Rust
194	   example the cargo cycle is:
195	   1. `cargo fmt`
196	   2. `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`
197	   3. `cargo test`
198	   4. `cargo install --path . --locked`
199	   5. (re-test if anything substantive changed)
200	5. **Work review.** Stop *before* writing any description;
201	   tell the user "ready to commit." The user reviews the
202	   changes and we iterate until complete.
203	6. **Write the commit description** — see
204	   [Commit description](#commit-description).
205	7. **Commit Description review.** Show the title + body
206	   and stop. The user reviews the description. Iterate.
207	8. **Commit.** `jj commit -m "title" -m "body" -R .` for
208	   the app repo, `-R .claude` for the bot repo (`-R` last
209	   keeps the verb visible):
210	
211	   ```
212	   jj commit -m \
213	   "<type>: <short description>" \
214	   -m "<intro paragraph>
215	
216	   - file1: gist
217	   - file2: gist
218	
219	   ochid: /.claude/<chid>" \
220	   -R .
221	   ```
222	
223	**Two overrides apply:**
224	
225	- **Deviation or question** — any time the work deviates
226	  from the agreed plan, or a question arises, stop and
227	  surface it; don't push through.
228	- **ESC-ESC** — the user can interrupt at any point to pull
229	  a review or question forward.
230	
231	## Commit description
232	
233	[Conventional Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/):
234	
235	```
236	<type>: <short description>
237	```
238	
239	Titles carry **no trailing `(<version>)` suffix**. The
240	version-of-record — where it lives and its bump cadence, see
241	[versioning.md](versioning.md) — is useful for confirming
242	you're running the version you're testing, not a per-commit
243	title marker. We think per-commit version-in-title is
244	unstable on large projects: many changes are in flight
245	simultaneously, and a
246	version is only stable once merged into the main repo — so
247	titles don't carry one.
248	
249	### Title
250	
251	- ≤50 chars total.
252	- Common types: `feat`, `fix`, `refactor`, `test`,
253	  `docs`, `chore`.
254	- Favor terse phrasings.
255	
256	### Body
257	
258	[Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form) (intro + bullets),
259	wrap ≤72. Bullet content differs per repo:
260	
261	- **App-repo body**: file-by-file. One bullet per file
262	  changed (file plus a one-line gist). Sub-bullets for
263	  files with multiple distinct changes:
264	
265	  ```
266	  - path/to/file1
267	    - first distinct change
268	    - second distinct change, wrapping to next line
269	       with continuation indented 5
270	  ```
271	
272	  The file-by-file list is the source of truth for the
273	  cycle's mechanical change record. Chores carries the
274	  narrative + design, not a copy of it. Promote any
275	  "why" beyond one sentence to a chores `###` subsection.
276	
277	- **Session-repo (`.claude`) body**: bullets describe
278	  in-session activity rather than code changes.
279	
280	### Trailer
281	
282	`ochid:` as the last line of the body — see
283	[Cross-repo linking (ochid trailers)](../AGENTS.md#cross-repo-linking-ochid-trailers)
284	in AGENTS.md for the convention.
285	
286	For breaking changes, use the hyphenated `BREAKING-CHANGE:`
287	trailer key. `BREAKING CHANGE:` (with a space) is the only
288	space-separated key the Conventional Commits spec allows; the
289	hyphenated form is also valid and avoids the space ambiguity.
290	
291	## Reviewing changes
292	
293	Work review looks at the **uncommitted working-copy diff**,
294	on the way to commit. The user opens diffs in their
295	editor (Zed, VSCode); jj commands are for terminal:
296	
297	- `jj diff` — working-copy diff (uncommitted)
298	- `jj diff -r @-` — diff of the previous commit
299	- `jj diff --from <X> --to <Y>` — any two revisions
300	- `jj show -r <X>` — description + diff for one rev
301	
302	Don't `jj edit -r @-` to view a past commit — that marks
303	it mutable and shifts `@`; use `jj diff -r @-` or
304	`jj show -r @-`.
305	
306	See [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders) for the
307	close-out squash recipe and recovery; revset primitives
308	are in [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets).
309	
310	## Pushing
311	
312	### Policy
313	
314	Push is **discretionary** during the cycle (backup,
315	progress visibility) and **mandatory at close-out** —
316	the cycle's result must be published.
317	
318	**Approval is per-push.** Every push — any repo, any kind:
319	cycle push, interim backup, recovery/surgery force-push —
320	happens only after the user has reviewed the changes to be
321	published and explicitly approved that specific push.
322	Approval of a plan that *includes* a push does not authorize
323	the push itself; stop and ask again at the moment of pushing.
324	
325	**Default is interactive; an explicit scoped delegation waives
326	the gates.** The gates above — per-push approval, the
327	commit-description review (show title+body and stop), and the
328	hard stop after push/finalize — are the *interactive default*.
329	They yield when the user **explicitly** delegates a complete,
330	bounded task and authorizes carrying it through ("do all of X
331	and push each step, don't check in"). The bot then proceeds
332	through that task's commits and pushes without stopping, and
333	continues past each push to the next step. Conditions:
334	
335	- **Explicit grant** — never inferred from a task merely being
336	  well-scoped; the user's words must authorize unattended
337	  completion.
338	- **Bounded goal** — covers the named task only; does not carry
339	  to the next task or a vaguer follow-on.
340	- **Destructive ops still pause** — delegation covers the task's
341	  ordinary commits and pushes; it does *not* pre-authorize a
342	  genuinely irreversible action (force-push over published
343	  history, history rewrite, deleting a remote branch). Those can
344	  permanently destroy work and aren't a normal cycle step, so the
345	  bot flags one before acting. An ordinary delegated cycle never
346	  reaches this.
347	- **Still transparent** — report each commit/push as it lands
348	  (title + outcome) so the user can catch up.
349	- **When in doubt, ask** — ambiguous authorization falls back to
350	  per-push approval.
351	
352	### Shape at close-out push
353	
354	At close-out the cycle's *work* is done; its *published
355	shape* is the remaining choice, made at push time. Surface
356	the options and get user approval before pushing — once on
357	the target, changing shape is a remote rewrite (force-push,
358	needs approval), so choose deliberately.
359	
360	- **Squash to one commit** — single entry on the target.
361	  Right for straightforward changes where the Work-N is
362	  focused on one or two files.
363	- **Merge non-ff** *(current default)* — `main` gains a
364	  merge commit (`X.Y.Z`); cycle commits stay reachable via
365	  two parents. `jj log -r ..@ -n <N>` shows the trapezoidal
366	  shape. See [Merge non-ff recipe](#merge-non-ff-recipe)
367	  for the full setup sequence.
368	- **Keep separate** — one commit per cycle entry on
369	  `main`. Use when the decomposition itself is
370	  informative. Each chores section keeps its own header /
371	  `Commits:` ref; no consolidation churn.
372	
373	Set up squash/merge before invoking `vc-x1 push`; use
374	`jj git push` directly for non-standard shapes.
375	
376	### Merge non-ff recipe
377	
378	Setting up a [Merge non-ff](#shape-at-close-out-push)
379	close-out is a fixed sequence. `<closeout>` is the cycle's
380	close-out commit, `<prev>` the previous cycle's close-out
381	(the current `main` tip), `<work-tip>` the cycle's last
382	Work commit:
383	
384	1. **Rebase the close-out into a merge** — `jj rebase -r
385	   <closeout> --onto <prev> --onto <work-tip>`.
386	   - `-r <closeout>` keeps the `<closeout>` commit in place.
387	   - `--onto <prev>` becomes its first parent (trunk).
388	   - `--onto <work-tip>` becomes the second parent.
389	
390	   Together these make `<closeout>` a merge of
391	   `<prev>` + `<work-tip>`, forming a trapezoidal commit.
392	2. **Use `jj new <merge>`** to add an empty `@` above the
393	   merge. The rebase left `@` *on* the now-content-bearing
394	   merge, which git/IDE diff views show as uncommitted;
395	   `jj new` restores the clean empty `@` on top.
396	3. **Push** — `jj git push --bookmark main -R .`.
397	
398	**Post-hoc caveat.** If the cycle was already pushed
399	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) its commits are
400	immutable: the rebase needs `--ignore-immutable` and the
401	push force-updates `main`.
402	The standard sequence assumes the merge is set up *before*
403	the close-out push.
404	
405	### vc-x1 push wrapper
406	
407	`vc-x1 push <bookmark>` wraps per-push mechanics. See
408	`vc-x1 push --help` for current flags.
409	
410	**Current limitation**: only fully supports the
411	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) shape; other
412	shapes need manual jj steps. Improvements tracked /
413	planned:
414	
415	- N:1 code↔bot for Merge non-ff (`## Todo` entry P1).
416	- Symmetric squash (planned, to be captured in `-2`).
417	- Per-repo bookmark names (planned).
418	
419	### .claude cadence
420	
421	**Cadence**: one push = one `.claude` commit, paired
422	with every code commit in that push.
423	
424	The `.claude` working copy accumulates session data
425	across the cycle; its change ID stays stable across
426	snapshots, `jj describe`, and the finalize commit, so
427	code-side `ochid:` trailers resolve.
428	
429	`.claude` is a linear journal — all session work lives
430	on `main`, regardless of the app-side bookmark. **Do not
431	create or maintain `.claude` bookmarks that mirror
432	app-side branches** — risks the bot steering session
433	pushes to the wrong remote ref.
434	
435	Ending a session: if the user runs `/exit` there will be
436	session information created, which we don't worry about.
437	The user can close the terminal instead and `@` will
438	remain empty.
439	
440	### Bot communication at the reviews
441	
442	Use plain prose — no insider jargon ("Gate N signal",
443	"Checkpoint N", etc.):
444	
445	- **At Work review** — summarize what changed and stop.
446	  "Work complete. Please review."
447	- **At Commit Description review** — present `$TITLE`
448	  and `$BODY` explicitly; ask permission to commit/push.
449	  Don't spell out the full `vc-x1 push ... --title ...
450	  --body ...` invocation by default.
451	- **At Post close-out review** — surface the shape
452	  options (squash / merge / keep) and the push target;
453	  wait for the user's choice before any `jj squash` /
454	  `jj rebase` / `jj git push` invocation.
455	
456	### After push or finalize: stop and wait
457	
458	After you **push** (cross the remote boundary) or launch
459	**finalize** on the `.claude` repo (`vc-x1 finalize --repo
460	.claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach`) — by
461	hand or via the `vc-x1 push` wrapper — you **MUST NEVER**
462	proceed (next step, edit, tool call, text output) until the
463	user explicitly directs you to continue. **Even when the next
464	step seems obvious — wait.** Treat push-or-finalize as a hard
465	stop for the whole turn.
466	
467	`vc-x1 push` performs both as its tail stages — `push-app`
468	then the detached `vc-x1 finalize` on `.claude` — so there is
469	no gap left to speak in once the push is invoked. Put **all**
470	closing words *before* invoking it. The harness rejects an empty
471	turn, so it may force a visible token after the tool returns; if
472	so, emit a bare acknowledgment only (e.g. "landed") — never a
473	summary, verification, or next-step offer.
474	
475	Why (`.claude` is the live journal finalize snapshots →
476	squashes → pushes):
477	
478	- **`--detach`**: lets the tool return immediately so the
479	  harness can keep flushing the *trailing* session-data — the
480	  tool call's own transcript and any words emitted *before*
481	  the invoke — into the snapshot.
482	- **`--delay 10`**: gives the harness time to complete that
483	  flush before the snapshot is taken. It is **not** time for
484	  the bot to do more work.
485	- **silence**: the bot must not summarize or perform any work
486	  after the tool returns. The harness may force a visible token
487	  (it rejects an empty turn) — keep it to a bare acknowledgment,
488	  never a summary or a next step.
489	
490	**Known slip**: the bot has emitted a summary after launching
491	finalize. There is no "harmless" closing line — if it's worth
492	saying, say it *before* the invoke.
493	
494	### Recovery
495	
496	- **If push exits before `finalize-claude`** (e.g.
497	  failure between `push-app` and `finalize-claude`), run
498	  finalize by hand:
499	
500	  ```
501	  vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach --log /tmp/vc-x1-finalize.log
502	  ```
503	
504	- **Run finalize again if `@` is non-empty** after the
505	  finalize's squash-and-commit (also desirable after
506	  extra activity by the bot's agents).
507	  - Why: finalize snapshots `.claude` at `--delay`, but the
508	    bot keeps writing session data while it runs — the tool's
509	    own invocation plus the bot's closing response land
510	    *after* the snapshot.
511	  - Safe to repeat: bot session data is append-only, so a
512	    re-run never conflicts or overwrites. (This could
513	    change; it is not under the user's control.)
514	  - Fix: run `vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push
515	    <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach` another time to capture
516	    that tail; one extra pass is generally enough.
517	  - No guarantees: events outside the bot's control can leave
518	    `@` non-empty — e.g. the bot's back end may decide to
519	    squash/consolidate session data, which can take minutes
520	    and land after the snapshot. The remedy is the same: just
521	    run finalize again. This is why a single pass is never
522	    guaranteed to leave `@` empty.
523	- **Clear push's saved state** after any out-of-band
524	  recovery — `rm .vc-x1/push-state.toml` or `vc-x1 push
525	  <bookmark> --restart` — otherwise push resumes from a
526	  stale stage.
527	- **Late code-repo tweak after `push-app` succeeded**
528	  (e.g. updating AGENTS.md or memory) requires `jj
529	  squash --ignore-immutable` and a re-push; that is a
530	  remote rewrite and needs explicit approval like any
531	  push.
532	
533	## Iterative work
534	
535	When work for a single commit (the **target**) benefits
536	from incremental review, loop:
537	
538	1. `jj new -R .` — fresh empty `@` on top of the target.
539	2. Make the next round of changes.
540	3. User reviews the round (see
541	   [Reviewing changes](#reviewing-changes)).
542	4. `jj squash -R .` folds into the target and creates a
543	   new empty `@`.
544	5. If not done, go to step 2.
545	
546	Same jj mechanics as a
547	[sub-cycle ladder](#sub-cycle-ladders), but at
548	single-commit scope — the version
549	doesn't change.
550	
551	## Sub-cycle ladders
552	
553	When a Work commit subdivides into a sub-cycle (see
554	[Numbering](#numbering) for suffix nesting), its Work
555	commits typically live as a local jj `@` chain and
556	**collapse into the sub-cycle's Close-out** before the
557	parent cycle continues. Ladder commits are scratch —
558	for review and bisection only.
559	
560	### Per-Work-commit contract within a ladder
561	
562	For each Work commit in the ladder:
563	
564	1. `jj new -R .` — create a fresh empty `@`.
565	2. Do the commit's work.
566	3. Run the fast validation (Rust example: `cargo test
567	   --bins`). **Non-negotiable** — for code, build and clippy
568	   alone miss regressions until a later commit runs the full
569	   suite, raising bisection cost.
570	4. `jj describe -m "..." -m "..." -R .` — working title
571	   only; the sub-cycle Close-out collects everything
572	   into one final commit.
573	
574	### Navigating the ladder
575	
576	Common moves:
577	
578	- `jj log -r '<base>::' -R .` — see the whole ladder
579	  from its base.
580	- `jj edit -r <prefix> -R .` — jump `@` to any ladder
581	  commit by chid prefix; useful for bisection.
582	- `jj edit @-- -R .` — quick-jump back two commits.
583	- `jj diff -r <chid> -R .` — review one commit in
584	  isolation.
585	
586	Modifications to any ladder commit rewrite it in place;
587	descendants auto-rebase.
588	
589	### Close-out: squash the ladder
590	
591	When all ladder Work commits are done and tests pass:
592	
593	```
594	jj squash --from "<base>..@-" --into @ -u -R .
595	```
596	
597	`<base>` is the parent of the first ladder commit; `-u`
598	keeps `@`'s description and discards the sources'.
599	After squash, history is linear: `<base> → @`;
600	intermediate commits are auto-abandoned.
601	
602	Then `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` as for any other commit.
603	
604	For N = 1 the squash is a no-op (`<base>..@-` is empty
605	when `@-` is `<base>`); push the single commit directly.
606	
607	### Recovery
608	
609	If a ladder commit goes wrong, back out without losing
610	prior commits:
611	
612	- **Discard the current commit.** `jj abandon @ -R .`
613	  drops it; you get a fresh empty `@` on the same
614	  parent.
615	- **Edit an earlier commit.** `jj edit -r <chid> -R .`,
616	  make corrections, then `jj edit -r <last-ladder-chid>`
617	  to return. Descendants auto-rebase.
618	- **Discard the entire ladder.** `jj op log -R .` shows
619	  the op history; `jj op restore <op-id> -R .` reverts
620	  to that point. Full undo — removes *all* ladder work
621	  after the chosen op. Use only to start over.
622	
623	# References
624	
625	- [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets) — revset primitives
626	  (chid/cid, `@`/`@-`/`@+`, `..`/`::` ranges, prefix matching).
627	- [`substep-test.sh`](substep-test.sh) — script that
628	  scaffolds a 4-revision ladder under `/tmp/substep-test`
629	  for squash-recipe experiments.
630	- The per-commit `cargo test --bins` gate exists because a
631	  regression introduced in an early ladder commit can go
632	  uncaught until a later commit runs the full suite, raising
633	  bisection cost.
634	
```

1	# Versioning
2	
3	How this project versions its commits and the running
4	artifact. The scheme — the cycle step-numbering and the
5	unique-per-commit aim — is generic and shared across projects:
6	this file is copied **verbatim**, with
7	[Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
8	covering each medium by conditional rather than per-project
9	edits.
10	
11	## Terms
12	
13	Three names, used as defined here across
14	[AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md),
15	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md), and the notes files:
16	
17	- **version** — the per-commit version (e.g. `0.3.0-5.3.0`),
18	  written with `-` in prose, ladders, chores, todo, and commit
19	  talk; its suffix encodes the cycle phase (see
20	  [Step numbering](#step-numbering)).
21	- **version-of-record** — the authoritative stored copy of the
22	  version, in the project's manifest (see
23	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record));
24	  a built or running artifact derives from it.
25	- **versioning** — the topic: this scheme as a whole.
26	
27	## Recording the version-of-record
28	
29	Where the version-of-record lives, how it's stored and
30	surfaced, and how often it changes — pick the case that fits
31	your medium:
32	
33	- **Manifest** — where the version-of-record is stored:
34	  - if Rust, `Cargo.toml` `[package].version`
35	  - if Python, `pyproject.toml` `[project].version` (or the
36	    committed config it's sourced from)
37	  - otherwise wherever the medium records it (a generic
38	    `version.toml`, a book's frontmatter, …) — add the case as
39	    needed
40	- **Notation** — how the `-` form is stored:
41	  - if the format allows `-` (TOML `version.toml`, `Cargo.toml`),
42	    store it verbatim
43	  - if it bars `-` (PEP 440's local segment, e.g. a Python
44	    project), remap to `+` — `0.3.0-5.3.0` → `0.3.0+5.3.0`: same
45	    version, just the stored spelling
46	- **Reporter** — how a built artifact surfaces the
47	  version-of-record:
48	  - if a CLI app, `<cli-app> -V`
49	  - if a TUI/GUI, add to Help/About or display on the title
50	- **Cadence** — how often to bump: see
51	  [Unique per commit](#unique-per-commit-preference-not-requirement);
52	  this project follows the per-commit preference.
53	
54	## Unique per commit (preference, not requirement)
55	
56	Our general notion is that the version-of-record should change
57	on **every commit**, so a built or running artifact identifies
58	the exact commit it came from.
59	
60	- This is a preference, **not** a hard requirement. A project
61	  following the cycle protocol may bump less often — once per
62	  cycle, only at release, and so on. Record the choice in
63	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
64	  if it differs.
65	- It is achievable because the cycle's versions (below) are
66	  **pre-assignable**, unlike the git SHA, which a commit cannot
67	  contain (see the cycle protocol's
68	  [Commits backfill](cycle-protocol.md#commits-backfill)).
69	
70	## Step numbering
71	
72	The cycle (Preparation -> Work -> Close-out; see
73	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md)) encodes each commit's
74	phase in the version suffix — the **final identifier `0` marks
75	a Preparation**:
76	
77	- `X.Y.Z-0` — Preparation
78	- `X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, … — Work commits
79	- `X.Y.Z` — Close-out (bare version, no suffix)
80	
81	**Preparation is optional.** A lightweight cycle — no ladder,
82	no setup commit — skips `-0` and starts at `-1` (its first
83	Work commit). The same holds at every level: a sub-cycle
84	needing no Preparation omits its `.0` (see Nesting). One that
85	grows a Preparation later adds the `0` step without
86	renumbering siblings.
87	
88	Disambiguation:
89	
90	- `-10` — Work commit #10 (final identifier `10`), not a
91	  Preparation.
92	- `-1.0` — Preparation of the `-1` sub-cycle (final
93	  identifier `0`).
94	
95	**Nesting.** Sub-cycles append another level, recursively:
96	
97	- `X.Y.Z-3.0` — Preparation of the `-3` sub-cycle
98	- `X.Y.Z-3.1`, `X.Y.Z-3.2` — its Work
99	- `X.Y.Z-3` — its Close-out
100	- `X.Y.Z-3.1.0` — Preparation of the `-3.1` sub-sub-cycle
101	
102	Bump the version-of-record at the start of each phase, so the
103	active phase is recorded — and, per the preference above,
104	every commit carries a distinct version.
105	
```

Confirmed: bare `set-bin` (no `=`) → `BinSet::Enable`, which in lib.rs:226 does `(on_port, r.mask)` — keeps the existing mask, just sets the async port.

**What "re-enable the current mask" means:** register 75 holds a *Common-group field bitmask* (the `r.mask` — which fields stream: time, accel, gyro, …). "Re-enable the current mask" = a bare `set-bin` (no `=fields`) turns binary streaming back **on using whatever field set is already stored in reg 75**, without redefining the fields. The mask persists in the register even when streaming is off (`set-bin=off` sets the port/mode to 0 but leaves `r.mask` intact), so after `set-bin=off` a later bare `set-bin` resumes the *same* fields. It's the "resume what I had" verb. You're right that it's binary-specific and belongs in the `set-bin` row below, not the shared intro.

> AGENT

Hold on — I want to flag that the suggested wording would be inaccurate, before I write it. A bare `set-bin` doesn't read flash, and it doesn't update the fields:

- It sends `VNRRG,75` to read the **current register** (the volatile working copy — lib.rs:213), keeps the existing field mask `r.mask` unchanged, and sets the output port to turn streaming back **on** (lib.rs:226 `BinSet::Enable => (on_port, r.mask)`).
- Flash never enters this path. Flash is touched only by `--persist` (a `VNWNV` save), and the register is *loaded* from flash only at power-on/reset. A runtime bare `set-bin` reads the live register, not flash.

So "Updates the fields ... from the value in flash" is wrong twice over: it reads the volatile register (not flash), and it doesn't change the fields (it re-enables output of the fields already set). If the register currently has, say, time+accel and you `set-bin=off` then bare `set-bin`, you get time+accel streaming again — same fields, just switched back on.

Accurate alternatives for that line — pick one (or steer me):

1. *"Bare `set-bin` resumes output of the fields already configured."*
2. *"Bare `set-bin` re-enables streaming without changing the fields (e.g. after `set-bin=off`)."*
3. Keep "re-enables the current mask" but gloss *mask* → *"re-enables the current field set."*

Which do you want? (Or if you actually intended a *new* behavior — read the field config back from flash into the register — that's a feature change, not a doc tweak, and a different conversation.)

> DEVELOPER

Glad we touched this because my interpretation was wrong. You have to explain more preciely what is being "re-enabled" and where is it coming from if I do a `set-bin=time,ypr` and then a bare `set-bin` what is the result?

> AGENT

Good — let me trace your exact scenario through the code precisely. The key fact: **"the current mask" lives in the device's volatile register 75, not in the tool and not (at runtime) in flash.** Every `set-bin*` command starts by reading it back with `VNRRG,75` (lib.rs:213).

**Step 1 — `set-bin=time,ypr`:**
- `value="time,ypr"` → `BinSet::Fields` (cli.rs:382). `parse_fields` orders by bit → time (bit 0), ypr (bit 3), so the mask is `(1<<0)|(1<<3)` = `0x0009` (lib.rs:228).
- It reads the current register, then writes `VNWRG,75,<port>,<divisor>,01,0009` — enabling output on the port (port 2 if binary was off, else the current port) at the existing rate divisor (lib.rs:223,234,238).
- Net: register 75 now holds **mask `0x0009` (time+ypr), streaming on, at the prior rate**. Volatile — no flash write without `--persist`.

**Step 2 — bare `set-bin`:**
- `value=None` → `BinSet::Enable` (cli.rs:380).
- It reads register 75 back: that read returns `r.mask = 0x0009`, `r.async_mode = 2` (it's currently streaming), `r.divisor` = the current rate.
- `BinSet::Enable => (on_port, r.mask)` (lib.rs:226): it keeps `r.mask` unchanged and sets the port. Since streaming is already on, `on_port = r.async_mode` (lib.rs:223), so it writes `VNWRG,75,2,<divisor>,01,0009` — **byte-for-byte the same register it just read.**

**Result of your scenario: nothing changes.** Binary keeps streaming time+ypr at the same rate. The bare `set-bin` is an idempotent rewrite here, because streaming was already on.

The bare `set-bin` only *does* something when binary is currently **off**:
- `set-bin=time,ypr` → streaming time+ypr.
- `set-bin=off` → `BinSet::Off => (0, r.mask)` (lib.rs:225): sets mode 0 (streaming off) but **leaves the mask `0x0009` sitting in the register**.
- bare `set-bin` → reads mask `0x0009` (still there), `on_port = 2` (because async_mode is 0 → lib.rs:223), writes it back with output enabled → **resumes streaming time+ypr.**

So "re-enable the current mask" precisely means: *re-read whatever field set is still sitting in register 75 and turn its output back on.* It's the "resume after `set-bin=off`" verb. Two sharp edges worth knowing:

- **It never reads flash.** The mask source is the live volatile register — which is the last `set-bin=…` value, or (only at power-on/reset) the flash default.
- **Re-enabling always targets port 2** (the RPi5 TTL header), regardless of which port it was on before the `off`, because `on_port` defaults to 2 whenever `async_mode == 0` (lib.rs:223). If you'd been streaming on port 1, `off`, then bare `set-bin`, it comes back on **port 2**, not port 1.

Given that, the most accurate one-liner for the `set-bin` row is something like:

> *"Bare `set-bin` resumes binary output of the fields still in the register (use after `set-bin=off`); it re-enables on port 2."*

Want me to use that (or a trimmed version), and should I also note the port-2 detail in the help, or leave that as code-level nuance?

> DEVELOPER

I suggest we add `set-bin=on` and a `set-bin=time,ypr` just sets the fields doesn't change on or off. (we should probably do `set|=time,ypr` which or's in the new fields and `set^=time,ypr` toggles just kidding but it makes sense to a programmer). So if it's on or off `set-bin=time,ypr` just redefines the fields. It it's off a `set-bin=time,ypr set-ascii=off set-bin=on` would do what you expect in that order. Thoughts?

> AGENT

Love it — this is the right instinct, and it's a real improvement, not a doc tweak. The core idea is **orthogonality**: make "what fields" and "is it streaming" independent knobs instead of conflating them. Today `set-bin=time,ypr` secretly also flips streaming on, which is exactly the hidden side effect that made "re-enables the current mask" confusing in the first place. Thoughts, in order:

**The model is clean and already half-built.** You'd end up with three orthogonal knobs on register 75:
- `set-bin=<fields>` — redefine the field mask, leave on/off untouched
- `set-bin=on` / `set-bin=off` — toggle streaming, leave the mask untouched
- `set-bin-hz=<hz>` — set the rate (already orthogonal — it just writes the divisor)

That's consistent: all three "set" one thing and only that thing. `set-bin-hz` already behaves this way, so this brings `set-bin` into line with its sibling.

**It composes perfectly with the `0.3.0-6` step grammar.** Your example `set-bin=time,ypr set-ascii=off set-bin=on` *is* a single-connection multi-step line — which is exactly what `-6` builds (shell-word steps, one port open, left-to-right). The orthogonal verbs are what make that line read declaratively. So I'd fold this into `-6` (or a small dedicated step right before it) rather than treat it as separate.

**Edge cases to pin down before coding:**
- **Enable target port.** When `set-bin=on` flips from off, the register's `async_mode` is 0, so we've *lost* which port it was on — today's code defaults to port 2 (RPi5 TTL, lib.rs:223). With orthogonal on/off that default becomes more visible: `on port1 → off → on` silently lands on port 2. Either keep port-2-default (document it) or store/restore the last port. Port 2 is right for this rig, so I'd document it and move on.
- **`set-bin=on` with an empty mask** (never configured, or mask 0) → the device rejects a zero-field frame with `$VNERR`. Guard it: "no fields configured — `set-bin=<fields>` first."
- **Bare `set-bin`** — make it an alias for `set-bin=on` (least surprise) or drop it with a hint. I'd alias it.
- **Backward compat** — the README/`--help` examples (`set-bin=time,accel,gyro` then `set-bin-hz=200`) currently rely on the implicit enable; they'd need a trailing `set-bin=on`. Fine since we're mid-redesign of the whole grammar, but it's a docs sweep.

**Symmetry with `set-ascii`.** For consistency the same split wants to apply to ASCII: `set-ascii=<preset>` redefines, `set-ascii=on`/`off` toggles. Right now `set-ascii=off` exists but a preset implicitly enables — same conflation. Worth deciding now whether to do both verbs together (consistent, slightly bigger) or just `set-bin` first. I'd do both so the grammar is uniform.

**`|=` / `^=`:** the toggle is a joke, agreed, but `set-bin+=time` (OR-in a field) is genuinely sensible and cheap to parse later. I'd note it as a future nicety and keep the first cut minimal — `=`, `on`, `off` only. YAGNI until someone wants incremental field edits.

**Implication for the in-flight docs commit (`0.3.0-5.1`):** good news — it's untouched by this. We only edited the *intro* paragraph (generic, survives the redesign) and the `AGENTS.md` semicolon rule. We never edited the `set-bin` row's "re-enables the current mask" line — and now we *shouldn't*, because this redesign will rewrite it. So the wording question that kicked this off resolves cleanly: don't tighten that line, change the behavior instead. The docs commit stays as-is and lands independently.

So my recommendation: **finalize the `0.3.0-5.1` docs commit as it stands now**, and I'll capture "decompose `set-bin`/`set-ascii` into orthogonal fields/on/off verbs" as a `## Todo` (or a `-6` design note in chores-01), to land with the step grammar. Want me to proceed to the commit description for `0.3.0-5.1`, and draft the Todo entry for the orthogonal-verbs redesign?

101	## Registers 75/76/77 — Binary Output 1/2/3 — ICD §3.2.8–3.2.10 (registers), §2 (message format); vnsdk `BinaryOutput1/2/3`
102	Write fields: `asyncMode` (serial-port bitmask), `rateDivisor`, then a field mask
103	per selected group. **Output rate = 800 / rateDivisor** (800 Hz IMU base; so
104	`rateDivisor 4` → 200 Hz). ✓ Three independent outputs, each its own rate.
105	
106	### Common Group (group byte `0x01`) — ICD §2.2 (Table 2.3)
107	Bit offsets within the Common field mask, and on-wire sizes:
108	
109	| bit | field | content | bytes | tool name |
110	|---|---|---|---|---|
111	| 0 | TimeStartup | `u64` ns | 8 | `time` ✓ |
112	| 2 | TimeSyncIn | `u64` ns | 8 | — |
113	| 3 | Ypr | 3×`f32` deg | 12 | `ypr` ✓ |
114	| 4 | Quaternion | 4×`f32` | 16 | `quat` ✓ |
115	| 5 | AngularRate | 3×`f32` rad/s | 12 | `gyro` ✓ |
116	| 8 | Accel | 3×`f32` m/s² | 12 | `accel` ✓ |
117	| 9 | Imu | UncompAccel(12)+UncompGyro(12) | 24 | `imu` |
118	| 10 | MagPres | Mag(12)+Temp(4)+Pres(4) | 20 | `magpres` |
119	| 11 | Deltas | DeltaTheta+DeltaVel | 28 | — |
120	| 13 | SyncInCnt | `u32` | 4 | — |
121	
122	Sub-field sizes per ICD §2.4 (UncompAccel/UncompGyro = 12 each; Pressure = 4).
123	Frame length = `1 (sync) + 1 (groups) + 2 (field mask) + payload + 2 (CRC)`.
124	Code: `FIELDS` (`bench --bin --fields`).
125	
126	## Error responses — ICD §1.5 (Table 1.6, $VNERR); vnsdk `Errors.hpp` `Error`
127	`$VNERR,<code>*XX` — `<code>` is **hex**.
128	
129	| code | meaning | | code | meaning |
130	|---|---|---|---|---|
131	| 0x01 | hard fault | | 0x08 | invalid register |
132	| 0x02 | serial buffer overflow | | 0x09 | unauthorized access |
133	| 0x03 | invalid checksum | | 0x0A | watchdog reset |
134	| 0x04 | invalid command | | 0x0B | output buffer overflow |
135	| 0x05 | not enough parameters | | **0x0C** | **insufficient baud rate** |
136	| 0x06 | too many parameters | | 0xFF | error buffer overflow |
137	| 0x07 | invalid parameter | | | |
138	
139	`0x0C` is the common one here: too much data for the current baud. Code:
140	`error_description()` / `vnerr_message()`.
```

> AGENT

Confirmed against `REFERENCE.md` — so here's the precise answer on **other registers for this syntax:**

- **Registers 76 & 77 (Binary Output 2/3)** — structurally *identical* to 75 (`REFERENCE.md:101`): same `asyncMode` / `rateDivisor` / group-mask layout, just two more independent outputs. The entire `set-bin` grammar (`=fields`, `=on/off`, `+=`/`-=`, `set-bin-hz`) generalizes to them verbatim — the tool would only need a way to target output 2/3.
- **Reg 75's own `asyncMode`** is itself a **serial-port bitmask** (`REFERENCE.md:102`) — which ports the output streams on. That's another set-valued field, so `+=`/`-=` (add/remove a port) or per-port on/off is the same syntax. This is exactly the "enable defaults to port 2" nuance from before.
- **Other output groups within 75** (Common is just group `0x01`; Time/IMU/Attitude/… each have their own mask) — if the tool ever grows past Common-only, each group's mask is another `+=`/`-=` target.
- **Not `set-ascii`** — reg 6 is a single-valued *preset* enum (off/ypr/qtn/…), so `=on`/`=off` fits but `+=`/`-=` does not (you can't OR presets). The set-arithmetic is specifically for the bitmask registers.

**Drafted todos** (added to `notes/todo.md` as ranks #2 and #3, below the zero-parse bug #1 — easily reorderable):

> **2.** Decompose `set-bin` (and `set-ascii`) into orthogonal verbs: a field/preset write that never toggles streaming, plus explicit `=on`/`=off`. Today `set-bin=<fields>` secretly enables output too, conflating "what fields" with "whether it streams". Lands with the step grammar so `set-bin=time,ypr set-ascii=off set-bin=on` reads declaratively. Pin down: `=on` defaults to port 2, an empty-mask guard before `=on`, and bare `set-bin` as an `=on` alias. [[1]]
>
> **3.** `set-bin+=<fields>` / `set-bin-=<fields>`: OR-in / mask-out Common fields incrementally. Generalizes to the other bitmask registers — Binary Output 2/3 (regs 76/77) and reg 75's `asyncMode` port mask — but not `set-ascii` (reg 6 is a single-valued preset). Builds on the orthogonal `set-bin` verbs. [[1]]

```
vc-x1 0.65.2-0

Dual-repo commit+push+finalize in one resumable command.

Collapses today's manual Commit-Push-Finalize Flow into a
single subcommand with two interactive approval gates and a
state machine with persistent progress so interruptions can
resume without re-doing completed stages.

Stages: preflight (fmt/clippy/test) → review (approve diff)
→ message ($EDITOR / --title+--body, approve text) →
commit-app → commit-claude (skipped if clean) → bookmark-both
→ push-app → finalize-claude. Failures in commit-app /
commit-claude / bookmark-both roll both repos back via
`jj op restore` to the snapshot recorded before commit-app.
After push-app succeeds the remote boundary is crossed and
recovery is forward-only.

Non-interactive use: pass both --title and --body plus --yes
to skip the review gate. Saved state carries title/body
across resumes so only the first invocation needs them.

Usage: vc-x1 push [OPTIONS] [BOOKMARK]

Arguments:
  [BOOKMARK]
          Bookmark to advance in both repos (positional form of `--bookmark`).
          
          Accepting a positional lets the common case read as `vc-x1 push main`
          without the `--bookmark` ceremony; `--bookmark` is kept as an alias
          for scripts and for composition with other args. The two forms
          conflict if both supplied.

Options:
      --bookmark <BOOKMARK>
          Bookmark to advance in both repos (flag form; see positional)

  -V, --version
          Print the `vc-x1 X.Y.Z` banner as the first line, then continue. With
          no subcommand, prints the banner and exits.
          
          Replaces clap's auto-version (which would exit after printing): the
          banner now rides along with normal subcommand execution rather than
          gating it, so scripts can capture the version *and* the command's
          output in one invocation.

      --restart
          Clear any saved state file and start from stage 1

  -v, --verbose...
          Verbose output: -v debug, -vv trace

      --from <STAGE>
          Explicit stage to jump to (advanced / debug use)

          Possible values:
          - preflight:       Run fmt / clippy / test / install / retest
          - review:          Present diff for the first approval gate
          - message:         Compose / edit the commit message; present for
            second gate
```

            second gate
          - commit-app:      Commit the app repo
          - commit-claude:   Commit the `.claude` session repo (skipped if
            empty)
          - bookmark-both:   Advance both bookmarks to `@-`
          - push-app:        `jj git push --bookmark <b> -R .`
          - finalize-claude: `vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <b>
            ...`

      --log <LOG>
          Log file path (captures all levels)

      --step
          Pause between every stage for an interactive approval gate

      --status
          Print where the saved state thinks we are and exit

      --recheck
          Re-run `preflight` even on resume (default: skip if last run
          succeeded)

      --no-finalize
          Stop before `finalize-claude` so it can be run manually

      --dry-run
          Print the exact commands for every stage without side effects

      --title <STR>
          Commit title (skip `$EDITOR` for the message stage)

      --body <STR>
          Commit body (skip `$EDITOR` for the message stage)

  -y, --yes
          Auto-approve interactive prompts (review gate, message-edit
          confirmation). Required when `--title` / `--body` aren't both supplied
          in non-interactive contexts

  -h, --help
          Print help (see a summary with '-h')

> AGENT

Pulled the preset table (`REFERENCE.md:74`, `cli.rs:22`). ASCII presets are whole canned messages, so "fewer fields" = a leaner preset. Rough on-wire sizes (sign+3int+dot+3frac ≈ 9 B/float, plus `$VNxxx,…*XX\r\n` framing), and what each costs at 40 Hz on the 11,520 B/s link:

| preset | floats | ~bytes/msg | @40 Hz | % link |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `ymr` (current) | 12 | ~121 | 4,840 B/s | 42% |
| `mar` / `yba` / `yia` | 9 | ~93 | 3,720 B/s | 32% |
| `qtn` | 4 | ~47 | 1,880 B/s | 16% |
| **`ypr`** | **3** | **~38** | **1,520 B/s** | **13%** |

`ypr` (yaw/pitch/roll only) is the natural minimal attitude preset. Dropping `ymr`→`ypr` frees ~3,300 B/s, and that's exactly what lets binary run at the **200 Hz** fc.py target alongside ASCII:

- **ASCII `ypr` @40 Hz + binary `time,accel,gyro` @200 Hz**: 1,520 + (38 B × 200) = 1,520 + 7,600 = **9,120 B/s ≈ 79%** of the link. Fits, with margin — clearly not a saturation case.
- Conservative fallback: same but binary **@100 Hz** → 5,320 B/s ≈ **46%**.

Commands (the orthogonality wrinkle is moot on the current tool — `set-ascii=ypr` both selects and streams since reg 6 has no separate enable bit, and `set-bin=<fields>` enables binary on port 2):

```
rw-vn100 set-ascii=ypr --baud 115200
rw-vn100 set-bin=time,accel,gyro --baud 115200
rw-vn100 set-bin-hz=200 --baud 115200
rw-vn100 bench --baud 115200 2
```

One caveat that matters for the **actual cold-open hunt** (not for this first check): these writes are volatile, so a power cycle reverts to `ymr` + binary-off. To reproduce the *cold-open* zero-parse with both streams live at the first open, we'd need to `--persist` the dual config and then power-cycle, so the very first `bench` is the cold open against both streams. For right now, volatile is fine — we just want to confirm both streams coexist at 115200.

Two quick calls before we go:
1. **Binary rate** — start at **200 Hz** (the real target, 79%) or the conservative **100 Hz** (46%)?
2. **Who runs it** — you at the rpi5 console (as you did with the earlier benches), or want me to run them via Bash here? (If you run it, I'd avoid touching the port to prevent contention.)

472	## fix: bench silences async before binary config
473	
474	Commits: [[5]]
475	
476	`bench --bin` could reject a binary config with `$VNERR` 0x0C
477	("insufficient baud rate") at a low baud even when the binary stream
478	fits the link with room to spare. The cause was write ordering:
479	bench configured the binary output (reg 75) while ASCII async (reg
480	7) was still streaming, so the device's fit check saw the *combined*
481	ASCII + binary load. The fix silences ASCII async before writing the
482	binary config, so the check sees only the binary load.
483	
484	### Bench combined-load fit check at low baud
485	
486	Observed 2026-06-26 on the RPi5 TTL header (`/dev/ttyAMA0`) running
487	`bench --bin` after an in-session baud switch to 57600:
488	
489	- At 57600 with ASCII async still at 40 Hz, `VNWRG,75,2,20,01,0101`
490	  (time+accel, 26 B/frame, 40 Hz) was rejected with `$VNERR` 0x0C.
491	- The binary stream alone is ~10 kbit/s — 18% of the 57600 link —
492	  so it fits easily; only the transient ASCII-40 + binary-40
493	  combination overran the check.
494	- Lowering ASCII async to 4 Hz first, then re-running the identical
495	  bench, the same `VNWRG,75` was accepted and streamed clean at
496	  39.9 Hz. Only the ASCII rate changed, isolating the combined load
497	  as the cause.
498	
499	We think the device enforces `message_size × rate ≤ baud` on every
500	reg-5 / reg-7 / reg-75 write, against the *sum* of the streams
501	configured on that port. At 115200 the headroom hid the transient;
502	at 57600 it surfaced. The ordering was always latent and only became
503	visible at the lower baud.
504	
505	The fix reorders `bench_binary`:
506	
507	- Before: read reg 7 → write reg 75 → write reg 7=0. The fit check
508	  fired on the reg-75 write, before ASCII was silenced.
509	- After: read reg 7 → write reg 7=0 → write reg 75, and restore reg
510	  7 if the reg-75 write still fails — so a genuinely-too-big binary
511	  frame (e.g. the 38 B frame at 200 Hz / 115200, [[2]]) no longer
512	  leaves ASCII async switched off.
513	- `bench_ascii` is unaffected: it has a single stream and already
514	  writes the message type (reg 6) before the rate (reg 7).
515	
516	## Rename rdwr_vn100 to rw-vn100

> AGENT

Good catch, and I checked the surface for the mechanism: **there's no single global async toggle** in the tool (or the documented register set — no `VNASY`-style pause). Async is **per-stream**:

- **ASCII** — `set-ascii=off` (reg 6 → 0); re-enable with `set-ascii=ypr` (rate in reg 7 is preserved, so it comes back at 40 Hz).
- **Binary** — `set-bin=off` (reg 75 `asyncMode` → 0, mask retained); re-enable with bare `set-bin` or `set-bin=<fields>`.

The old bench's "silence" was exactly this: write reg 7 → 0 before touching reg 75. And the chores section behind that fix confirms *why it matters beyond contention*: **the device runs its `size × rate ≤ baud` fit-check against the *combined* load of all streams on the port, on every reg-5/7/75 write** (chores-01:499). So writing the binary config while ASCII streams makes the device evaluate both together. At 115200 there's headroom, but quieting first is the clean, correct order regardless.

So for change 2, the minimal-contention sequence (I'd run these three; the reg-75 write lands on a quiet wire):

```
set-ascii=off                  # silence ASCII
set-bin=time,accel,gyro        # write reg 75 quiet; binary starts at 40 Hz
set-ascii=ypr                  # re-enable ASCII
```

Then you `bench 2` → expect `ypr` @40 Hz + binary @40 Hz.

**One honest limitation, and it's literally Todo #2:** the current `set-bin=<fields>` *also enables* (no way to define the mask while keeping binary off), so the binary turns on the instant I write it — meaning during that final `set-ascii=ypr` write, binary is already streaming. We can't get a fully-silent "configure everything, then flip on" with today's verbs. The orthogonal `set-bin=on/off` redesign is exactly what would let us stage all the writes quiet and enable last. For now, this three-step is as quiet as it gets.

Want me to run those three, then you bench? Or would you rather I also `set-bin=off` between steps to keep each individual write quiet — at the cost of more reopens?

> DEVELOPER

Seems we should add set-bin=off/on to make this test work?

> AGENT

Agreed — and it's the right call for a sharper reason than convenience. We're about to hunt an *intermittent* bug, and the cardinal rule there is to keep the test harness deterministic so the tool's own behavior isn't a confound. Right now `set-bin=<fields>` enables on the same write that sets the mask, so the finicky reg-75 write can never happen on a fully quiet wire. Decoupling that removes config-time contention as a variable before we start measuring. It's also already-planned work (Todo #2) — we'd just be pulling the binary half forward because the test needs it.

**The actual change** (binary only — ASCII already toggles cleanly via `set-ascii=off` / `set-ascii=ypr`, so it doesn't need this):

- `set-bin=<fields>` — set the mask, **preserve** current on/off (`asyncMode`) instead of force-enabling. *(behavior change)*
- `set-bin=on` — enable: set the output port, keep the mask. *(new)*
- `set-bin=off` — unchanged (disable, retain mask).
- bare `set-bin` — alias of `=on`.
- Guard: `set-bin=on` with an empty mask → error *"no fields configured — `set-bin=<fields>` first"* (avoids the device's zero-field `$VNERR`).
- Enable still defaults to port 2 (RPi5 TTL), as today.

That makes the clean test sequence:

```
set-ascii=off              # quiet
set-bin=time,accel,gyro    # mask only — stays OFF, fully quiet reg-75 write
set-bin=on                 # binary on
set-ascii=ypr              # ascii on
```

Only the two enables touch a live wire, and the sensitive field write is silent.

**It's a real `feat:` commit**, not a quick edit — touches `lib.rs` (the `SetBin` match arm), `cli.rs` (parse `on`, update the `set-bin` help row), the README examples (which rely on the old auto-enable), and the `SetBin` tests. So I'd run it as a proper cycle commit: implement → fmt/clippy/test → show you the diff → commit.

Two things to confirm before I touch code:
1. **Version** — I'd land it as `0.3.0-5.2` (keeps the planned `-6` step-grammar work distinct), and trim Todo #2 down to the remaining parts (ASCII symmetry, `+=`/`-=`). Good, or would you rather it *be* the start of `-6`?
2. **Scope** — binary-only now, ASCII orthogonality stays in the Todo? (I think yes — ASCII off/on already works.)

Once you confirm, I'll implement and show you the diff before committing.

> DEVELOPER

I suggest, for now, "bare set-bin" is an error.
How will you know if "no fields are configured" will you read the register 75 first?

> AGENT

Both good. Two answers:

**Bare `set-bin` → error: yes, and it's the consistent choice.** Bare `set-ascii` is *already* an error in the tool (cli.rs:509 — "reg 6 has no separate enable bit"), so making bare `set-bin` an error too keeps the two verbs symmetric and forces the intent to be explicit (`=on` / `=off` / `=<fields>`). So `BinSet::Enable` becomes reachable only via `set-bin=on`.

**The empty-mask check — yes, we read register 75 first, and we already do.** Every `set-bin*` command is a read-modify-write: it issues `VNRRG,75` up front (lib.rs:213) and parses the reply into `r` (`async_mode`, `divisor`, `mask`). So `set-bin=on` gets the current mask *for free* from that same read — no extra round-trip. The guard is then just:

- `parse_reg75` returns `mask = 0` whenever the Common group bit is clear or the field mask is literally zero (proto.rs ~217) — i.e. "no Common-group fields configured."
- In the `BinSet::Enable` arm, if `r.mask == 0` → error `"no fields configured — set-bin=<fields> first"`, before we write anything.

The device stays the final arbiter — any *other* rejection (e.g. `$VNERR 0x0C` if a future config doesn't fit the baud) still surfaces through the existing write-path error handling. The mask guard is just a friendly pre-check for the common "forgot to set fields" mistake, costing nothing extra since the read already happened. And in our test sequence the read lands on a quiet wire (ASCII silenced, binary off), so it's clean.

Updated plan, then:
- `set-bin=<fields>` — set mask, preserve on/off
- `set-bin=on` — enable (error if `r.mask == 0`)
- `set-bin=off` — disable, retain mask
- bare `set-bin` — **error** (needs `=<fields>`, `=on`, or `=off`)
