```json
  "old_string": "            // Read-modify-write reg 75: preserve the rateDivisor, change only the\n            // streaming port and/or the Common field mask per the action.\n            let cur = transact_retry(\n                &mut port,\n                &build_command(\"VNRRG,75\"),\n                5,\n                |l| parse_reg75(l).is_some(),\n                &no_reply,\n            )?;\n            let r = parse_reg75(&cur).unwrap(); // OK: predicate ensured Some\n            // Enabling defaults to port 2 (the RPi5 TTL header) when binary is\n            // currently off; otherwise it keeps whichever port is already set.\n            let on_port = if r.async_mode == 0 { 2 } else { r.async_mode };\n            let (mode, mask) = match action {\n                BinSet::Off => (0, r.mask),\n                BinSet::Enable => (on_port, r.mask),\n                BinSet::Fields(fields) => {\n                    let m = fields.iter().fold(0u16, |acc, f| acc | (1u16 << f.bit));\n                    (on_port, m)\n                }\n            };\n            // A never-configured output can read back divisor 0; fall back to\n            // 40 Hz so the first enable yields a valid, low-rate stream.\n            let divisor = if r.divisor == 0 { 20 } else { r.divisor };\n            if mode != 0 && divisor != r.divisor {\n                println!(\"(binary rate was unset; defaulting to 40 Hz — use set-bin-hz to change)\");\n            }",
  "new_string": "            // Read-modify-write reg 75. Fields and on/off are orthogonal: a\n            // field write preserves async_mode, on/off preserves the mask; both\n            // preserve the rateDivisor.\n            let cur = transact_retry(\n                &mut port,\n                &build_command(\"VNRRG,75\"),\n                5,\n                |l| parse_reg75(l).is_some(),\n                &no_reply,\n            )?;\n            let r = parse_reg75(&cur).unwrap(); // OK: predicate ensured Some\n            let mut defaulted_rate = false;\n            let (mode, mask) = match action {\n                BinSet::Off => (0, r.mask),\n                BinSet::Enable => {\n                    // Enabling an empty mask would stream a zero-field frame the\n                    // device rejects; require fields first.\n                    if r.mask == 0 {\n                        return Err(\"no fields configured — set-bin=<fields> first\".into());\n                    }\n                    // Default to port 2 (the RPi5 TTL header) when currently off;\n                    // otherwise keep whichever port is already set.\n                    let on_port = if r.async_mode == 0 { 2 } else { r.async_mode };\n                    defaulted_rate = r.divisor == 0;\n                    (on_port, r.mask)\n                }\n                BinSet::Fields(fields) => {\n                    let m = fields.iter().fold(0u16, |acc, f| acc | (1u16 << f.bit));\n                    defaulted_rate = r.divisor == 0;\n                    (r.async_mode, m)\n                }\n            };\n            // A never-configured output can read back divisor 0; fall back to\n            // 40 Hz so the first enable yields a valid, low-rate stream.\n            let divisor = if r.divisor == 0 { 20 } else { r.divisor };\n            if defaulted_rate {\n                println!(\"(binary rate was unset; defaulting to 40 Hz — use set-bin-hz to change)\");\n            }"
}
```

> AGENT

Holding — I won't install or touch anything. Let me read that sequence back, because I think it's a baud mismatch, not a dropout:

- **17:03:28 run used `--baud 921600`** while the device is still at **115200** (we only set `ascii=ypr` — volatile — and never issued a `baud` command, so it's at the flash-default 115200). The host was listening 8× too fast, so the 115200 bytes are unintelligible → "none seen." The tool even diagnosed it: *"does --baud match its current rate?"* And the "~46 kbit/s = 5%" line confirms the wire was **active** (real bytes arriving, ~15 kbit/s of VNYPR misread as ~46 kbit/s of garbage at the wrong clock) — not silence, not a wedge.

So that third "no data" is fully explained by the `921600` argument, **not** the cold-open zero-parse bug. The bug signature we're hunting is no-data *at the correct 115200 baud* — and your two 115200 runs (16:29:17, 16:29:51) were both clean VNYPR at 39.6 Hz.

Also noted: you backed up the current binary to `rw-vn100.0.3.0-5.1` before I overwrite it — good call for A/B'ing old vs new. Status on my side: the `0.3.0-5.2` orthogonal-`set-bin` work is implemented in the working copy and passed fmt/clippy/test, but I have **not** installed it (your `cp` backup is intact) and have **not** committed it.

Waiting for note 2/2 before doing anything.

> DEVELOPER

I changed the baud, but it should have worked, correct?

> AGENT

No — it correctly showed no data, and here's the distinction that bit it:

- **`--baud 921600` did not change the device's baud.** That flag only sets the rate *your host* talks/listens at. The help spells it out: *"Baud to talk to the device NOW; must match the device's CURRENT rate."* It's a listening clock, not a command to the device.
- **`bench` is passive — it never writes register 5** (or anything). So nothing told the VN-100 to switch. It stayed at its flash-default **115200** (we only ever set `ascii=ypr`, volatile; no `baud` command was issued).
- So you had a 115200 talker and a 921600 listener — an 8× mismatch. The bytes arrive but are unintelligible → "none seen," with the ~46 kbit/s "throughput" being that misread garbage. Exactly what the tool's *"does --baud match its current rate?"* hint was pointing at.

The verb that actually changes the device baud is the **`baud`** command (it sends `VNWRG,05` and switches the live connection):

```
rw-vn100 baud 921600          # switch the DEVICE to 921600 (add --persist to keep it)
rw-vn100 bench --baud 921600  # now host and device match
```

Two cautions before you go that route, though:
- **Bug #1 (the wedge):** high-baud *fresh-process* reconnects at 921600 can wedge the UART, recoverable only by power cycle. That was on the RS-232 rig; on this TTL header it's untested (we think it may not reproduce on TTL) — but 921600 is precisely the risky baud.
- We deliberately picked **115200 as the reliable path** for this dual-stream / zero-parse work. Jumping to 921600 reintroduces the wedge as a confound. I'd stay at 115200 until the orthogonal `set-bin` test is done, then change device baud deliberately if we want to probe higher.

So: nothing actually failed there — just a host/device baud mismatch. Want to continue with note 2/2, or pick back up on installing/committing `0.3.0-5.2`?

> DEVELOPER

You're right, I didn't two step it, my bad. continue...

> AGENT

Fixed (comment-only; I'll re-run the cycle right before the push). Proposed commit:

```
feat: orthogonal set-bin verbs (fields vs on/off)

Decouple the binary field mask from streaming on/off so config
writes can land on a quiet wire — set the fields with output off,
then enable. Pulled forward from the verb-redesign Todo because the
dual-stream reliability test needs a contention-free setup.

- src/lib.rs: SetBin no longer enables on a field write. Fields
  preserves async_mode, Enable (=on) sets the port. Guard =on
  against an empty mask (would stream a device-rejected zero-field
  frame). The rate-defaulted note now fires on the field/enable
  paths.
- src/cli.rs
  - add `set-bin=on` parsing
  - make bare `set-bin` an error (enable is explicit now, like
    bare set-ascii)
  - help row → `set-bin=<FIELDS|on|off>`, plus configure→rate→on
    examples
  - update the BinSet doc and the parse test
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: version-of-record 0.3.0-5.1 -> 0.3.0-5.2.
```

One open item — **Todo #2's disposition**, which I have *not* touched yet. Implementing this surfaced a wrinkle: the ASCII half of that todo isn't actually achievable. Register 6 (ADOR) is a single value where `0 = off` and `N = preset-N streaming` — there's **no separate enable bit**, so you can't "set the preset while keeping it off" the way binary now allows. Making `set-ascii` orthogonal would require the tool to *remember* the last preset across invocations, which fights its stateless design.

So my read: **Todo #2 is effectively complete** — binary orthogonality landed here, ASCII is N/A by register design. I'd move it to `## Done` with that note, and keep `#3` (`+=`/`-=`) as the live follow-on.

Two questions before I push:
1. Agree to **close Todo #2** (binary done, ASCII N/A), or keep it open for a stateful "remember last preset" approach?
2. Fold that todo edit into **this** commit, or keep this commit code-only and do the todo bookkeeping separately?

2. fold in the todo edit this commit.

> DEVELOPER

This:

  set-bin=<FIELDS|on|off> Set the binary field mask, leaving streaming as-is.
                          `on` enables (needs fields), `off` disables.
                          Fields: time, ypr, quat, gyro, accel, imu, magpres

Reads like on and off control how the listed fields changed,
but I think the FIELDS replaces any current setting for the
fields and on|off enables and disables. Maybe we need:


  set-bin-fields=<FIELDS>
  set-bin=on|off

> AGENT

You're right — overloading `set-bin=<FIELDS|on|off>` makes `on`/`off` read like field operations, and it muddles a verb that should do one thing. Your split is cleaner and it matches the existing `set-bin-hz` pattern, so the three reg-75 aspects each get their own verb:

- `set-bin-fields=<FIELDS>` — the mask
- `set-bin-hz=<HZ>` — the rate
- `set-bin=on|off` — streaming toggle (bare `set-bin` still an error)

Since `0.3.0-5.2` isn't pushed yet, I'll fold this into it rather than ship the ambiguous form. Let me find every `set-bin` reference to update them all consistently.

```json
  "new_string": "/// What a binary-output config step does (register 75).\n///\n/// Fields and on/off live in separate verbs so they stay orthogonal\n/// — a mask write never toggles streaming, and a streaming toggle\n/// never touches the mask:\n///\n/// - `Fields` — `set-bin-fields=<list>`: set the Common field mask\n///   to these fields, leaving streaming on/off unchanged.\n/// - `Enable` — `set-bin=on`: enable streaming with the device's\n///   current mask (an error if no fields are configured).\n/// - `Off` — `set-bin=off`: disable streaming, leaving the mask\n///   configured."
}
```

> AGENT

Everything reads cleanly now — each verb is unambiguous, and `set-bin=time,accel` gives a helpful redirect to `set-bin-fields`. (The exit-2 is just the error-path commands; expected.)

Final commit, retitled to reflect the split:

```
feat: split set-bin into fields / on-off verbs

Give each reg-75 aspect its own verb so none is overloaded:
set-bin-fields sets the mask, set-bin-hz the rate, set-bin=on|off
the streaming toggle. The old set-bin=<FIELDS|on|off> conflated
mask edits with the on/off toggle. Decoupling also lets config
writes land on a quiet wire (set fields with output off, then
enable), which the dual-stream reliability test needs.

- src/lib.rs
  - SetBin no longer enables on a field write: Fields preserves
    async_mode, Enable (=on) sets the port
  - guard =on against an empty mask (a zero-field frame the device
    rejects)
  - the rate-defaulted note now fires on the field/enable paths
  - module-doc and error strings name the new verbs
- src/cli.rs
  - add `set-bin-fields=<FIELDS>` and restrict `set-bin` to on/off
    (bare or other value errors)
  - update help rows, examples, command list, BinSet doc, tests
- notes/todo.md
  - close the verb-split Todo to Done (binary done, ASCII N/A —
    reg 6 has no separate enable bit)
  - rename the increment Todo to `set-bin-fields+=`/`-=`
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: version-of-record 0.3.0-5.1 -> 0.3.0-5.2.
```

Five files: `src/lib.rs`, `src/cli.rs`, `notes/todo.md`, `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`. `ochid:` injected by the wrapper, target `main`, keep-separate.

Ready to push on your word.

> DEVELOPER

Clean, I like it, vc-x1 push

Decoupling also lets config\nwrites land on a quiet wire (set fields with output off, then\nenable), which the dual-stream reliability test needs.\n\n- src/lib.rs\n  - SetBin no longer enables on a field write: Fields preserves\n    async_mode, Enable (=on) sets the port\n  - guard =on against an empty mask (a zero-field frame the device\n    rejects)\n  - the rate-defaulted note now fires on the field/enable paths\n  - module-doc and error strings name the new verbs\n- src/cli.rs\n  - add `set-bin-fields=<FIELDS>` and restrict `set-bin` to on/off\n    (bare or other value errors)\n  - update help rows, examples, command list, BinSet doc, tests\n- notes/todo.md\n  - close the verb-split Todo to Done (binary done, ASCII N/A —\n    reg 6 has no separate enable bit)\n  - rename the increment Todo to `set-bin-fields+=`/`-=`\n- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: version-of-record 0.3.0-5.1 -> 0.3.0-5.2.'",
```json
  "description": "Commit and push 0.3.0-5.2 on main"
}
```

> AGENT

Dual stream confirmed at 115200, and the numbers land right on prediction:

- **ASCII:** 40.0 Hz VNYPR, 1519 B/s (est. ~1,520)
- **Binary:** 39.5 Hz, 38 B/frame, 1500 B/s, Common time/gyro/accel
- **Total:** ~30 kbit/s = 26% link — exactly as budgeted

And the binary decode is sane: gyro ≈ 0 (stationary), accel `[8.994, -2.605, -0.075]` ≈ gravity projected onto a heavily tilted frame (YPR pitch +74°, roll +88°) — consistent with the ASCII attitude. So the orthogonal-verb flow produced a clean, correct dual stream with the field write landing on a silent wire.

**But** — this is one *warm* run, so it's a config success, not a reproduction of the intermittent zero-parse. That bug bites *fresh-process opens* ("works on a re-run"), so the way to provoke it is many back-to-back fresh `bench` invocations. Two ways forward:

1. **Probe first (cheap, no code):** run a loop of fresh benches and count any `none seen` to confirm it still reproduces in this dual-stream config and get a rough rate. One-liner you can run and paste:
   ```
   for i in $(seq 1 20); do echo -n "run $i: "; rw-vn100 bench 1 --baud 115200 2>&1 | grep -E "ASCII:|Binary:" | tr '\n' ' '; echo; done
   ```
2. **Instrument first:** build the in-memory capture mode (the Todo #1 env-gated raw dump) *before* probing — so the moment a run fails, we've captured the bytes to diff against `test-data/both-streams.bin`. A transient failure with no capture is a wasted reproduction.

My rec: run the probe loop now to confirm it reproduces (and how often). If it fires even once in 20, we build the capture mode and re-probe with it armed. If 20/20 are clean, we rethink — maybe it needs cold power-cycle opens (persist the config + power-cycle), which is a different setup.

Want to run that loop and paste, or should I run it here?

> AGENT

The new line validates cleanly:

- ASCII 1513 B/s + Binary 1513 B/s = **3026 B/s** parsed
- Wire total = **3035 B/s**
- Gap = **9 B/s** (~18 bytes over 2.01s)

That ~0.3% gap is just measurement-boundary partials — a line or frame straddling the start/stop of the 2s window, counted in the raw byte total but not completed into a message/frame. So the wire is **clean**: essentially every byte is accounted for as ASCII or binary, no junk. Exactly the validation you wanted, and a healthy signal. (Binary decode still sane too — gyro ≈ 0, accel ≈ gravity on the tilted frame.)

This is a third clean run, though — dual stream is solid but the intermittent zero-parse hasn't shown. Time to actually provoke it with back-to-back fresh opens. Here's the probe loop again (now each run also shows the B/s gap, so a partial-stream failure is obvious):


> AGENT

**20/20 clean** — every run shows both streams at 40 Hz, no `none seen`, throughput steady. So the zero-parse did **not** reproduce in this config.

Reading the noise:
- The B/s split into two clusters (~1519 vs ~1494) is a **window-quantization artifact**, not a real rate change: each run captures exactly 40 messages, but elapsed time is 1.00 s vs 1.02 s, so dividing by the longer window gives the lower B/s. The device is rock-steady at 40 Hz.
- The wire gap is consistently ~15–16 B/s (one partial line/frame at the 1 s boundary). Clean every time.

**The telling clue is in Todo #1's own data:** the past failures cited "~269 kbit/s" throughput. That's impossible on a 115200 link (max ~115 kbit/s) — so *those failures happened at a higher baud* (269 kbit/s ≈ 29% of 921600). We think the intermittent zero-parse is tied to **higher baud / misframing** (the same family as Bug #1's RS-232 high-baud wedge), not a 115200 defect. That's consistent with 20/20 clean here.

But we've only tested **40 Hz** binary — and the fc.py goal is **200 Hz**. That's the config that actually matters, and at 5× the frame rate it stresses the parser far more. It still fits 115200: ypr ASCII (~1,520 B/s) + binary `time,gyro,accel` @200 Hz (38 B × 200 = 7,600 B/s) = ~9,120 B/s ≈ **79%** of the link. Same reliable baud, no wedge risk.

So I'd **bump binary to 200 Hz and re-run the 20× probe** — that's the meaningful reliability test for the goal. I'd do it as a quiet rate change (silence ASCII → `set-bin=off` → `set-bin-hz=200` → `set-bin=on` → `set-ascii=ypr`), then loop. If 200 Hz is also clean, 115200 is validated for the 200 Hz path; if it trips, that's the reproduction that matters.

**20/20 clean at 200 Hz — 40/40 across both probes, zero failures.** The zero-parse did not reproduce even at the full fc.py target rate and 79% link load.

Validation (the new B/s line earning its keep):
- Run 1: ASCII 1519 + binary 7594 = 9113 parsed vs 9119 wire → **6 B/s gap**. Every run's gap stays in the 6–16 B/s range — clean wire even at 79% utilization, no unparsed junk.
- The occasional 199-frame or 39-message run (e.g. run 18) is just the 1 s window catching one fewer at the boundary — the device holds 200.0 / 40.0 Hz steady.

**Conclusion:** binary 200 Hz + ASCII alongside at **115200 is reliable** — 40 fresh opens, 0 drops. That's the fc.py target config validated on the proven baud. And the intermittent zero-parse is **not a 115200 phenomenon**: we couldn't provoke it in 40 tries, and Todo #1's own "~269 kbit/s" failure can't exist on a 115200 link (max ~115 kbit/s), so those failures were at a higher baud — we think it's the same high-baud misframing family as Bug #1, not a distinct bug here.

So the device side is in good shape for the 200 Hz goal. Where to next:

1. **Record the finding** — update Todo #1 with this evidence (40/40 clean at 115200 incl. 200 Hz; symptom appears baud-dependent, likely overlapping Bug #1). Small notes commit. I'd recommend this regardless of what's next, so the result isn't lost.
2. **Move toward the fc.py 200 Hz patch** — the device path is now proven, so this is the natural next goal.
3. **Chase the bug at 921600** — only if you want to confirm the high-baud hypothesis, but that reintroduces the wedge risk and is off the reliable path.

> AGENT

Agreed — incremental is the right way to find the crossover, and there's a sharper framing that makes this experiment doubly worth it: **Bug #1's wedge was only ever seen on the RS-232 rig, and we noted "we think it may not reproduce on TTL."** We're on the TTL header now (`/dev/ttyAMA0`). So this sweep simultaneously hunts the zero-parse crossover *and* tests the TTL-immunity hypothesis. Two outcomes, both valuable:
- Clean all the way to 921600 → the wedge/misframe was RS-232-specific, and 921600 is usable on TTL (great news for headroom).
- Breaks at some baud → we've found the TTL crossover.

**Finding I'll record in Todo #1** (for your review before it lands):

> *Finding (2026-06-28):* 40/40 fresh-open benches clean at 115200 dual-stream (ASCII ypr 40 Hz + binary 200 Hz, 79% link) — the zero-parse does not reproduce at 115200. The cited ~269 kbit/s failure can't occur on a 115200 link (max ~115 kbit/s), so those failures were at a higher baud. We think this is the same high-baud misframing family as Bug #1, not a 115200 defect. Next: sweep baud 115200 → 230400 → 460800 → 921600 to find the crossover (also tests whether the wedge reproduces on TTL).

**Experiment mechanics** (a couple matter):
- The **in-session `baud` switch is clean** at every baud (per Bug #1) — it's the **fresh-process bench reopen** that wedges. So per level: `baud <next>` to switch the device, then N fresh `bench` reopens (that's the part under test). Keep the dual stream on (realistic; at higher baud it's a smaller % so easy).
- **Baud stays volatile — never `--persist`.** That's the safety net: any wedge recovers with a power cycle back to flash-default 115200 (you're at the rig). A persisted high baud could brick the link.
- **Classify each failure:** a *transient* zero-parse (re-run recovers) is the Todo #1 symptom; *persistent* silence at every baud is a Bug #1 wedge → stop and power-cycle. On the first failure I'll re-test before concluding.
- A power cycle resets our volatile config, so we'd reconfigure the dual stream after one.

**One choice before I start** — how to sweep:
- **Chained (no power cycles):** bump 230400 → 460800 → 921600 in one pass, ~10 reopens each. Fast, aggressive (cumulative stress — Bug #1 wedged at 230400 this way). Good for a quick "does TTL break at all."
- **Power-cycle-isolated:** clean power cycle before each baud, so each level's intrinsic rate is measured (Bug #1's controlled retest stayed clean to 921600 minus the top). Cleaner crossover, but you power-cycle between levels.

My rec: **chained first** (fast, finds if/where TTL breaks), then isolate around the crossover only if it breaks. Sound good, and ~10 reopens per level? Once you confirm, I'll record the finding and start the sweep.

> AGENT

Good question, but my vote is **not now** — and the reason is precise: the bug we're hunting *is* the fresh-process reopen. Separate `rw-vn100` invocations are exactly what trips it ("works on a re-run"), so a stream of independent `bench` opens is the right instrument. The `-6` step grammar's whole point is a **single** port open — running config + bench over one connection — which would *eliminate* the reopens and thus mask the very thing we're trying to provoke. For the hunt, we want the die-rolls, not fewer of them.

Two more reasons it's not needed right now:

- **We can already isolate reopen-vs-in-session without it.** The `baud` command switches *and verifies on the live connection* — that's an in-session read at the new baud (the path Bug #1 found clean). The subsequent fresh `bench --baud N` is the reopen (the suspect path). So if the switch+verify succeeds but the next `bench` fails, we've attributed it to the reopen — with the tools we have.
- **`-6` is a substantial feature** (shell-word steps, `+` join, single open, left-to-right). Building it now interrupts a live reproduction attempt to write code that would obscure the symptom.

Where `-6` *does* fit: it's the eventual **mitigation** — "each reopen is a wedge die-roll," so one connection = fewer die-rolls — and a sharper single-connection diagnostic *after* we know the crossover (e.g. `baud 921600 + bench` in one open vs reopen, to confirm the reopen is the TTL trigger). So I'd build it once we've characterized the bug, as the planned next step.

So: finish the chained sweep first with the current fresh-reopen probes. I've recorded the finding in Todo #1 (uncommitted) and I'm paused right before switching to 230400. Proceed with `baud 230400` + 10× probe, or do you want to pivot to `-6` first?

Do we want to add in-memory data capture, I think so, but you're welcome to persuade me otherwise.

> AGENT

You've basically persuaded me — but let me give you the honest counter-case first, then say why I land on yes.

**The counter (sweep-first):** we don't *know* the sweep reproduces yet, so we could sweep with the current probes, and only build capture once we've confirmed a failure baud — avoiding building an instrument we might not need. There's also a real limit: if the TTL failure turns out to be a *wedge* (silent), capture grabs nothing — empty buffer, no bytes to analyze. Capture only earns its keep on the *misframe* case (bytes arrive but don't parse).

**Why yes anyway:**
- Yesterday's failures were the **~269 kbit/s "none seen"** signature — that's misframe (bytes arriving), not silent wedge. So capture is squarely aimed at the failure mode we actually saw.
- The failure is **intermittent and precious**. If it trips during the sweep with no capture armed, we've burned a reproduction to learn only "it failed at baud N" — not *how* the bytes are mangled. Re-reproducing an intermittent bug to get the bytes is the expensive path.
- It's the **instrument Todo #1 already prescribes** ("capture a real failure via an env-gated raw dump and diff against `test-data/both-streams.bin`"). Unlike `-6`, it doesn't mask the bug — it's passive observation, and **byte-for-byte unchanged when the env var is unset**, so it can't perturb the experiment.
- It's **small and cheap**: a few lines in the bench read loop, in-memory `Vec<u8>`, RAM trivial (a 2 s capture is tens of KB even at 921600).

So: agree, build it first, then sweep with it armed.

**Proposed scope (`0.3.0-5.4`):**
- `RW_VN100_CAPTURE=<path>` env var. When set, `bench` appends every byte it reads off the wire into a `Vec<u8>` and writes it to `<path>` at the end of the run. Unset → no behavior change.
- Raw bytes, so it diffs directly against `test-data/both-streams.bin` (which is the same format).
- Scoped to `bench` (the passive dual-stream read — where the symptom lives).
- I'd fold the already-edited Todo #1 finding into this commit, since the instrument and the finding are the same investigation.

One open choice: **raw bytes only** (MVP, directly diffable) vs **also a sidecar of per-read timestamps/lengths** (lets us see *when* in the first second it goes wrong — useful for a reopen-timing bug, but more scope). I'd ship raw-bytes first and add timing only if the raw diff doesn't explain it.

Want me to build the raw-bytes MVP as `0.3.0-5.4`?

```
179	/// Passively read the live stream for `secs`, scanning the same bytes for ASCII
180	/// async lines and binary Common frames at once.
181	///
182	/// - ASCII: split on `\n`, count checksum-valid `$VN…` messages, keep the first
183	///   as a sample.
184	/// - Binary: accumulate bytes and pull out CRC-valid `0xFA` frames via
185	///   `try_common_frame`, keeping the first frame for a decoded sample.
186	/// - `total_bytes` counts every byte read, for the wire-utilization figure.
187	fn measure<S: Read>(port: &mut S, secs: u64) -> std::io::Result<Stats> {
188	    let start = Instant::now();
189	    let deadline = start + Duration::from_secs(secs);
190	    let mut buf = [0u8; 1024];
191	
192	    let mut total_bytes = 0u64;
193	
194	    // ASCII line-scanner state.
195	    let mut line: Vec<u8> = Vec::new();
196	    let mut msgs = 0u64;
197	    let mut ascii_bytes = 0u64;
198	    let mut ascii_sample: Option<String> = None;
199	
200	    // Binary frame-scanner state.
201	    let mut acc: Vec<u8> = Vec::new();
202	    let mut frames = 0u64;
203	    let mut bin_bytes = 0u64;
204	    let mut bin_mask: Option<u16> = None;
205	    let mut bin_first: Option<Vec<u8>> = None;
206	
207	    while Instant::now() < deadline {
208	        let n = match port.read(&mut buf) {
209	            Ok(0) => continue,
210	            Ok(n) => n,
211	            Err(ref e) if matches!(e.kind(), ErrorKind::TimedOut | ErrorKind::WouldBlock) => {
212	                continue;
213	            }
214	            Err(e) => return Err(e),
215	        };
216	        let chunk = &buf[..n];
217	        total_bytes += n as u64;
218	
219	        // ASCII view: walk bytes, completing a line on each `\n`.
220	        for &b in chunk {
221	            match b {
222	                b'\n' => {
223	                    let raw = String::from_utf8_lossy(&line);
224	                    let cand = match raw.rfind('$') {
225	                        Some(p) => raw[p..].trim(),
226	                        None => raw.trim(),
227	                    };
228	                    if is_async_line(cand) {
229	                        msgs += 1;
230	                        ascii_bytes += cand.len() as u64 + 2; // + CRLF
231	                        if ascii_sample.is_none() {
232	                            ascii_sample = Some(cand.to_string());
233	                        }
234	                    }
235	                    line.clear();
236	                }
237	                b'\r' => {}
238	                _ => {
239	                    line.push(b);
240	                    if line.len() > 1024 {
241	                        line.clear();
242	                    }
243	                }
244	            }
245	        }
246	
247	        // Binary view: accumulate, then pull out CRC-valid Common frames.
248	        acc.extend_from_slice(chunk);
249	        let mut i = 0;
250	        while i < acc.len() {
251	            if acc[i] != BENCH_SYNC {
252	                i += 1;
253	                continue;
254	            }
255	            match try_common_frame(&acc[i..]) {
256	                Sniff::Frame(flen) => {
257	                    frames += 1;
258	                    bin_bytes += flen as u64;
259	                    if bin_mask.is_none() {
260	                        bin_mask = Some(u16::from_le_bytes([acc[i + 2], acc[i + 3]]));
261	                        bin_first = Some(acc[i..i + flen].to_vec());
262	                    }
263	                    i += flen;
264	                }
265	                Sniff::NotHere => i += 1,
266	                Sniff::NeedMore => break, // partial frame at the tail; wait for more
267	            }
268	        }
269	        acc.drain(0..i);
270	        // Backstop: a stream with no valid frames never drains its tail prefix,
271	        // so cap the buffer (keep enough to span any single frame).
272	        if acc.len() > 8192 {
273	            let cut = acc.len() - 512;
274	            acc.drain(0..cut);
275	        }
276	    }
277	
278	    Ok(Stats {
279	        msgs,
280	        ascii_bytes,
281	        ascii_sample,
282	        frames,
283	        bin_bytes,
284	        bin_mask,
285	        bin_first,
286	        total_bytes,
287	        elapsed: start.elapsed().as_secs_f64(),
288	    })
289	}
290	
291	/// Print the passive bench result: ASCII rate, binary rate, wire throughput.
292	fn report(baud: u32, st: &Stats) {
293	    let secs = st.elapsed;
```

```
346	/// Passively measure the device's live output for `secs` seconds.
347	///
348	/// - Does no device writes — reports whatever is already streaming.
349	/// - Counts ASCII `$VN` async lines and binary `0xFA` Common-group frames
350	///   (CRC-checked) over the same bytes, plus total wire throughput against the
351	///   `baud` link.
352	pub fn bench<S: Read>(
353	    port: &mut S,
354	    baud: u32,
355	    secs: u64,
356	) -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
357	    println!("Measuring the live stream for {secs}s (passive — no device writes)...");
358	    let st = measure(port, secs)?;
359	    report(baud, &st);
360	    if st.msgs == 0 && st.frames == 0 {
361	        return Err(
362	            "saw no ASCII messages or binary frames — is the device streaming, \
363	             and does --baud match its current rate?"
364	                .into(),
365	        );
366	    }
367	    Ok(())
368	}
```

```
383	    impl Read for MockReader {
384	        fn read(&mut self, buf: &mut [u8]) -> std::io::Result<usize> {
385	            let n = (self.data.len() - self.pos).min(buf.len()).min(self.chunk);
386	            buf[..n].copy_from_slice(&self.data[self.pos..self.pos + n]);
387	            self.pos += n;
388	            Ok(n)

392	    /// Build a CRC-valid Common frame for `mask` with zeroed payload.
393	    fn test_frame(mask: u16) -> Vec<u8> {
394	        let payload: usize = fields_from_mask(mask).iter().map(|f| f.size).sum();
395	        let mut data = vec![BENCH_GROUPS, (mask & 0xff) as u8, (mask >> 8) as u8];
396	        data.extend(std::iter::repeat_n(0u8, payload));
397	        let crc = vn_crc16(&data);
398	        let mut frame = vec![BENCH_SYNC];
399	        frame.extend_from_slice(&data);
400	        frame.extend_from_slice(&crc.to_be_bytes()); // appended big-endian → CRC over frame == 0
401	        frame
402	    }
403

405	    /// 7-field, like the real 110-byte frame) each followed by an ASCII line.

407	        let frame = test_frame(0x0739); // all seven Common fields

409	        let ascii = format!("${payload}*{:02X}\r\n", checksum(payload));
410	        let mut data = Vec::new();

412	            data.extend_from_slice(&frame);
```

```
1	//! The `bench` command — passively measure whatever the device is already
2	//! streaming.
3	//!
4	//! - `bench` does **no** device writes: it reads the live serial stream for a
5	//!   fixed window and reports what it sees. No configure, no restore.
6	//! - The same bytes are scanned two ways at once — ASCII `$VN…` async lines
7	//!   (checksum-valid) and binary `0xFA` Common-group frames (CRC-valid) — plus
8	//!   a count of every byte off the wire for total throughput.
9	//! - Passive means the binary frame length is unknown up front, so each frame
10	//!   is sniffed from its own header (`try_common_frame`) rather than a config.
11	
12	use std::io::{ErrorKind, Read};
13	use std::time::{Duration, Instant};
14	
15	use crate::proto::*;
16	
17	// Binary frame: sync 0xFA, groups=0x01 (Common), one 16-bit field mask, the
18	// streamed fields' payload, then a 2-byte CRC. Length depends on the field set.
19	const BENCH_SYNC: u8 = 0xFA;
20	const BENCH_GROUPS: u8 = 0x01;
21	const BENCH_HEADER: usize = 1 + 1 + 2; // sync + groups + field mask
22	const BENCH_CRC: usize = 2;
23
```

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/nps-gnc/data/prgs/nps-gnc/rw-vn100/src/bench.rs",
  "old_string": "/// Passively measure the device's live output for `secs` seconds.\n///\n/// - Does no device writes — reports whatever is already streaming.\n/// - Counts ASCII `$VN` async lines and binary `0xFA` Common-group frames\n///   (CRC-checked) over the same bytes, plus total wire throughput against the\n///   `baud` link.\npub fn bench<S: Read>(\n    port: &mut S,\n    baud: u32,\n    secs: u64,\n) -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {\n    println!(\"Measuring the live stream for {secs}s (passive — no device writes)...\");\n    let st = measure(port, secs)?;\n    report(baud, &st);",
  "new_string": "/// A `Read` adapter that records every byte it passes through.\n///\n/// - Backs the `RW_VN100_CAPTURE` raw dump: the recorded bytes are exactly\n///   what the scanner saw, so the dump diffs directly against a known-good\n///   capture (e.g. `test-data/both-streams.bin`).\n/// - `inner` is the real port; `sink` accumulates the bytes in memory.\nstruct CaptureReader<'a, S: Read> {\n    inner: &'a mut S,\n    sink: Vec<u8>,\n}\n\nimpl<S: Read> Read for CaptureReader<'_, S> {\n    /// Read from `inner`, appending the bytes returned to `sink`.\n    fn read(&mut self, buf: &mut [u8]) -> std::io::Result<usize> {\n        let n = self.inner.read(buf)?;\n        self.sink.extend_from_slice(&buf[..n]);\n        Ok(n)\n    }\n}\n\n/// Passively measure the device's live output for `secs` seconds.\n///\n/// - Does no device writes — reports whatever is already streaming.\n/// - Counts ASCII `$VN` async lines and binary `0xFA` Common-group frames\n///   (CRC-checked) over the same bytes, plus total wire throughput against the\n///   `baud` link.\n/// - `RW_VN100_CAPTURE=<path>` set → records every raw byte read into memory\n///   and writes it to `<path>` at the end. Unset → the read path is unchanged.\npub fn bench<S: Read>(\n    port: &mut S,\n    baud: u32,\n    secs: u64,\n) -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {\n    println!(\"Measuring the live stream for {secs}s (passive — no device writes)...\");\n    let capture_path = std::env::var(\"RW_VN100_CAPTURE\")\n        .ok()\n        .filter(|p| !p.is_empty());\n    let st = if let Some(path) = capture_path {\n        let mut cap = CaptureReader {\n            inner: port,\n            sink: Vec::new(),\n        };\n        let st = measure(&mut cap, secs)?;\n        std::fs::write(&path, &cap.sink)?;\n        println!(\"Captured {} raw bytes to {path}.\", cap.sink.len());\n        st\n    } else {\n        measure(port, secs)?\n    };\n    report(baud, &st);"
}
```

> AGENT

The capture works end-to-end — and the dump is byte-perfect:

- **9136 bytes** captured ≈ the 9132 B/s throughput × 1 s (it records exactly `total_bytes`).
- Header decodes cleanly: `fa 01 21 01` = sync + Common group + mask `0x0121` (time/gyro/accel), and the next `fa` lands at offset 38 — the expected 38-byte frame spacing. ASCII `$VNYPR` lines are interleaved in between.

So this clean 115200 capture is now our **baseline** — when the sweep trips a failure, we capture it and diff against this. (Note: `test-data/both-streams.bin` is an older config — VNYMR + 7-field frames — so the real diff is failure-vs-good-capture-of-the-same-stream, which we now have.)

**Work complete — ready to commit.** Proposed `0.3.0-5.4`:

```
feat: bench raw-byte capture (RW_VN100_CAPTURE)

Add an env-gated in-memory capture so an intermittent zero-parse
can be caught and diffed against a known-good stream — the
instrument Todo #1 prescribes, ahead of the baud-crossover sweep.

- src/bench.rs
  - `CaptureReader` Read adapter records every byte read, and
    `bench` writes the buffer to `RW_VN100_CAPTURE` at exit
  - unset env leaves the read path byte-for-byte unchanged
  - test: capture records every byte of an interleaved stream
- src/cli.rs: note `RW_VN100_CAPTURE=<path>` in the bench help row
- notes/todo.md: record the Todo #1 finding (40/40 clean at 115200,
  higher-baud symptom, sweep next)
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: version-of-record 0.3.0-5.3 -> 0.3.0-5.4.
```

Five files: `src/bench.rs`, `src/cli.rs`, `notes/todo.md`, `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`. `ochid:` injected by the wrapper, target `main`, keep-separate.

Push on your word — then we run the chained sweep with capture armed.

> DEVELOPER

Let's preallocate the buffer in CaptureReader to minimze reallocations, I think we can estimate the size or pass it as a parameter?

> AGENT

Good call — pre-sizing keeps the read loop allocation-free, which also avoids perturbing timing during the capture (relevant when we're chasing a timing-sensitive bug). And we don't need a parameter: the wire has a hard physical ceiling — `baud/10` bytes/sec (8N1 = 10 bits/byte) — so `(baud/10) × secs` is a guaranteed upper bound. `bench` already has both, so I'll compute it there and pre-size the sink to exactly the link max (+ one read buffer for the final partial read past the deadline). That means zero reallocations, ever.

> TOOL

tool_use Edit
id: toolu_01BmEpYKCBFYd1FArxZaXjtM
```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/nps-gnc/data/prgs/nps-gnc/rw-vn100/src/bench.rs",
  "old_string": "    let st = if let Some(path) = capture_path {\n        let mut cap = CaptureReader {\n            inner: port,\n            sink: Vec::new(),\n        };",
  "new_string": "    let st = if let Some(path) = capture_path {\n        // Preallocate the link's physical ceiling for the window so the read\n        // loop never reallocates: baud/10 bytes/s (8N1 = 10 bits/byte) × secs,\n        // plus one read buffer for the final partial read past the deadline.\n        let cap_est = (baud as usize / 10) * secs as usize + 1024;\n        let mut cap = CaptureReader {\n            inner: port,\n            sink: Vec::with_capacity(cap_est),\n        };"
}
```

Updated commit (`0.3.0-5.4`):

```
feat: bench raw-byte capture (--capture <path>)

Add a `--capture <path>` flag so an intermittent zero-parse can be
caught and diffed against a known-good stream — the instrument
Todo #1 prescribes, ahead of the baud-crossover sweep.

- src/bench.rs
  - `CaptureReader` Read adapter records every byte read, and
    `bench` writes the buffer to the `--capture` path at exit
  - sink preallocated to the link ceiling (baud/10 × secs) so the
    read loop never reallocates
  - no path → read path byte-for-byte unchanged
  - test: capture records every byte of an interleaved stream
- src/cli.rs
  - `--capture <path>` global flag on `Command::Bench`, rejected
    for any verb but `bench`
  - help: bench row note + Global options entry
  - test: `--capture` parsing and the bench-only rejection
- src/lib.rs: pass the `--capture` path through to `bench`
- notes/todo.md: record the Todo #1 finding (40/40 clean at 115200,
  higher-baud symptom, sweep next)
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: version-of-record 0.3.0-5.3 -> 0.3.0-5.4.
```

Six files: `src/bench.rs`, `src/cli.rs`, `src/lib.rs`, `notes/todo.md`, `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`.

One unrelated thing I noticed while in the examples block: line ~288 still has a stale `rw-vn100 bench --bin --hz 200 --fields accel,gyro` example referencing flags removed in the passive-bench redesign — broken in `--help`. I left it alone to keep this commit focused, but flag it for a quick cleanup. Push this on your word.

> AGENT

`--capture` confirmed working end-to-end:

- **9136 bytes** written to `./tmp/capture.bin`, matching the wire throughput.
- It's the raw stream — note this dump *starts mid-frame* (bytes `30 c8 82 a0…` are the tail of a frame already in flight when bench opened), with the first full `fa 01 21 01` frame at offset 0x12. That's correct and expected: the capture grabs whatever's on the wire from the instant the port opens, and the scanner finds sync from there. So a failure capture will faithfully show the raw bytes, partial frames and all.
- Stream's still clean this run (40 Hz ASCII + 199 binary frames).

So the instrument is verified. Two small housekeeping notes before the sweep:
- I'd suggest adding `tmp/` to `.gitignore` so capture artifacts don't show up as untracked / risk being committed. Quick one-line change — want me to?
- For the sweep, I'll point `--capture` at a per-baud filename (e.g. `./tmp/cap-230400-run3.bin`) so a failure capture is unambiguous and we keep the clean 115200 one as the baseline to diff against.

Ready to start the chained sweep (230400 → 460800 → 921600), or add the `.gitignore` line first?

**460800: 10/10 clean** too — all fresh reopens held, and the ms timestamps are working (`114241066` = 11:42:41.066, all unique). Notable asymmetry: the marginality showed up in the *switch* transact (one retry, a command+reply round-trip) but not in the *passive bench reopens* — consistent with reads being more forgiving than transacts.

So 230400 and 460800 are both solid on TTL. **921600 is the danger zone** — it's where Bug #1 wedged even with power cycles (on RS-232). The in-session switch should survive (maybe with retries); the fresh-reopen probe is where a wedge would hit, and that needs a physical power cycle to recover. Proceeding with the switch to 921600:

> AGENT

**921600: 10/10 clean. Full sweep 30/30, no wedge, no zero-parse, no power cycles.** TTL held at every baud — 230400, 460800, and 921600 — with this config. That's a strong result on its own: the RS-232 wedge does **not** reproduce on TTL.

But here's the catch, and I think it's the real lead — **we under-loaded the test by ~3×.** Our config is 3-field binary (38 B/frame) + VNYPR = **~91 kbit/s**. Todo #1's failures were **~269 kbit/s**. Reverse-engineering that number:

- Full 7-field Common binary = 110 B/frame (the `0x0739` set, same as `test-data/both-streams.bin`). At 200 Hz = 22,000 B/s.
- VNYMR ASCII (~121 B) at 40 Hz = 4,840 B/s.
- **Total ≈ 26,840 B/s = 268 kbit/s** — almost exactly the "~269 kbit/s" failure.

So yesterday's failing config was almost certainly the **full 7-field binary + VNYMR**, not our lean one. And that matters doubly: **268 kbit/s can't fit under ~300k baud**, so that config *forces* baud ≥ 460800 — which is exactly why the failures were "at higher baud." It wasn't baud alone; it was a heavy config that only runs at high baud.

We think the bug is **data-rate dependent**, and we tested too light. To actually provoke it, match yesterday's load:

- `set-bin-fields=time,ypr,quat,gyro,accel,imu,magpres` (full, 110 B/frame)
- `set-ascii=ymr` (heavier ASCII)
- keep 200 Hz binary, stay at **921600** (268 kbit/s = 29% link there)
- re-probe 10× with capture armed

Device is currently at 921600 (volatile). Want me to reconfigure to the heavy 7-field + YMR stream and re-probe at 921600? That's the faithful reproduction of the ~269 kbit/s condition.

> AGENT

That note is the key — re-reading it, **we've been probing the wrong way.** The original hypothesis isn't "high baud" or "high data rate" per se; it's a **baud-sync issue on baud-*change* cold opens**:

> *"a 921600 open immediately following a 115200 open does not always apply the new baud (PL011 divisor), so the stream is read at the stale rate — misframed."* And for the full-throughput case: *"the port may come up bit-misaligned on some cold opens (the baud not fully applied)… re-opening clears."*

Two things that explain our 40/40:

1. **Our loops opened at the *same* baud repeatedly.** Within each 10× loop, the PL011 divisor was already locked from the prior run — only *run 1 of each level* was a true baud-change open (~4 total in the whole sweep). The trigger needs a baud *change* per open, and it's intermittent, so 4 chances wasn't enough.
2. **The passive bench reads cold.** The note points out the *old* bench ran a priming `transact_retry` before measuring (which forced the divisor to lock, or failed loudly); the passive bench just reads. The 150 ms settle+clear helps but isn't a guaranteed prime.

So the targeted reproduction is to **make every measured open a baud-change open** — alternate the host `--baud` so each 921600 read immediately follows a 115200 open, exactly the note's recipe. Device stays at 921600 (heavy config); the 115200 open just reads garbage to flip the PL011 divisor, then the 921600 open is the suspect, capture armed:

```
for i in $(seq 1 20); do
  rw-vn100 bench 1 --baud 115200 >/dev/null 2>&1          # wrong-baud open: flips PL011 to 115200
  TS=$(date +%H%M%S%3N)
  rw-vn100 bench 1 --baud 921600 --capture "./tmp/cap-alt-r${i}-${TS}.bin" \
    | grep -E "ASCII:|Binary:|Wire|none|error"            # the 921600-after-115200 suspect open
done
```

20 baud-change opens at 921600, each preceded by a 115200 flip — far more stress on the divisor re-application than the sweep gave it. If the misalignment is real, this should catch it, and `--capture` grabs the misframed bytes.

Run it?

> AGENT

This is the root cause, and the bytes prove it. Three-way comparison:

| run | size | structure | parse |
|---|---|---|---|
| **r5 clean** | 26900 | starts `fa 01 39 07` (valid sync+groups+mask `0x0739`), 438 `FA` / 88 `$` | full |
| **r4 full-misframe** | 26900 | frame-*shaped* (`fa 01 39 07` header, time field intact) but values corrupted → **every CRC/checksum fails** → 0 parsed | none |
| **r8 low** | 2248 | no structure — 1 `FA`, 3 `$` in the whole file → undersampled garbage | none |

So both Todo #1 modes are now reproduced, captured, and explained — and it's **host-side, not the device** (the stream never changed; only specific opens fail, and the next open clears it):

- **Low case (r8, r16):** the 921600 open kept the **stale 115200 divisor** → it sampled the 921600 stream 8× too slow → ~2250 bytes of garbage. Exactly the note's "~24 kbit/s after a wrong-baud open."
- **Full-misframe case (r4, r11):** the divisor applied at ~921600 (full byte count) but **bit-misaligned** → frame-shaped data that fails CRC. The note's "full ~269 kbit/s, none parsed."

We think the mechanism is the **RPi5 PL011 UART not reliably applying the new baud divisor on a fresh open when the baud differs from the previous open** — sometimes it stays stale (low), sometimes it locks misaligned (full-misframe). ~20% per baud-change open (4/20), and a clean re-open always fixes it — which is why it reads as "intermittent, works on re-run."

This also confirms *why the old bench was immune*: its pre-measure `transact_retry` was a write→read round-trip that forced the divisor to settle (or failed loudly); the passive bench reads cold.

Three next steps, in order of value:
1. **Preserve evidence** — copy one `full-misframe` and one `low` capture into `test-data/` (they're in gitignored `./tmp/`), as durable repro samples to diff against and to seed a regression.
2. **Record the finding** — update Todo #1 / chores-01 with the confirmed root cause, the recipe (alternate `--baud 115200`/`921600`), and the 4/20 rate.
3. **Design the fix** — host-side: re-open-on-bad-open (detect 0-valid/low-throughput in the first window and reopen, since reopen clears it), or re-assert the termios baud after open. This is the real Todo #1 fix.

Want me to start with (1) preserving the captures, then we write up (2)?

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/nps-gnc/data/prgs/nps-gnc/rw-vn100/test-data/zero-parse/repro.sh",
  "content": "#!/usr/bin/env bash\n# Reproduce the passive-bench zero-parse (Todo #1; analysis in\n# notes/chores/chores-01.md \"Zero-parse root cause: PL011 baud-change\n# open\").\n#\n# Mechanism: on the RPi5 PL011 UART, a fresh open whose baud differs\n# from the previous open does not always apply the new divisor —\n# sometimes it stays stale (undersampled garbage), sometimes it locks\n# bit-misaligned (full byte count, every CRC fails). So we make every\n# measured open a baud *change*: a wrong-baud `--baud 115200` open\n# (the device stays at 921600) immediately before each captured\n# `--baud 921600` read. ~20% of those opens fail; the streaming device\n# never changes and the next open always parses clean.\n#\n# Device prep (volatile — a power cycle reverts to flash 115200). From\n# the device's current baud <CUR>, set 921600 + a heavy 7-field stream:\n#   rw-vn100 baud 921600 --baud <CUR>\n#   rw-vn100 set-ascii=off --baud 921600\n#   rw-vn100 set-bin=off --baud 921600\n#   rw-vn100 set-bin-fields=time,ypr,quat,gyro,accel,imu,magpres --baud 921600\n#   rw-vn100 set-bin-hz=200 --baud 921600\n#   rw-vn100 set-bin=on --baud 921600\n#   rw-vn100 set-ascii=ymr --baud 921600\n#\n# Usage: repro.sh [OUT_DIR] [N]   (defaults: ./tmp, 20)\n# Failures print \"ASCII: none seen. Binary: none seen.\"; their capture\n# files hold the raw bytes. The committed triplets in this directory\n# (misframe-*, stale-*) are one captured instance of each mode.\nset -u\nOUT=${1:-./tmp}\nN=${2:-20}\nmkdir -p \"$OUT\"\nfor i in $(seq 1 \"$N\"); do\n  rw-vn100 bench 1 --baud 115200 >/dev/null 2>&1   # wrong-baud flip: device is at 921600\n  ts=$(date +%H%M%S%3N)\n  printf \"run %2d: \" \"$i\"\n  rw-vn100 bench 1 --baud 921600 --capture \"$OUT/cap-alt-r${i}-${ts}.bin\" 2>&1 \\\n    | grep -E \"ASCII:|Binary:|none|error\" | tr '\\n' ' '\n  echo\ndone\n"
}
```

Now the coinage is grounded everywhere it appears:

- **chores-01 (definition):** *"Passive `bench` intermittently reads bytes off the wire but parses **zero** of them into ASCII messages or binary frames — *zero-parse* — so it reports `ASCII: none / Binary: none`, then works on a re-run."*
- **Todo #1 (entry gloss):** *"…intermittent zero-parse — bytes read off the wire but zero parsed (`ASCII: none / Binary: none`)."*
- **repro.sh (header gloss):** *"…the passive-bench zero-parse — a full byte read off the wire that parses into zero messages/frames…"*

So a reader hitting the term in any of the three spots gets "bytes in, zero parsed" immediately, and chores-01 is the canonical definition. (This is the key distinction from a plain dropout: it's *not* silence — bytes do arrive, they just don't parse — which is exactly what makes the misframe/stale-divisor modes the story.)

Same file set as before, just with these gloss edits folded in. Does the wording read well? If so, I'll finalize: bump `0.3.0-5.5`, run the cargo cycle, and show the commit body for the push.

> DEVELOPER

In the script the first run is at 115200 and the comment says "wrong-baud flip" on a default configed device that isn't the case the script should probably do a `baud 921600` and then a quick test that it works prior to going into the test loop, or maybe I'm mixed up, likely :)

> AGENT

You're not mixed up — you're right. The script assumes the device was *already* prepped to 921600 (via the manual steps I'd buried in the header), so on a default 115200 device the first `--baud 115200` open is the *correct* baud, and the "wrong-baud flip" comment is false. The fix is to make the script self-configuring: set 921600 + the heavy stream, sanity-check a clean read, *then* the stress loop — at which point the 115200 flip genuinely is wrong-baud. Rewriting it:

> TOOL

tool_use Write
id: toolu_0131vHGFB5qHexaNQoCcH2qU
```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/nps-gnc/data/prgs/nps-gnc/rw-vn100/test-data/zero-parse/repro.sh",
  "content": "#!/usr/bin/env bash\n# Reproduce the passive-bench zero-parse — a full byte read off the\n# wire that parses into zero messages/frames (Todo #1; analysis in\n# notes/chores/chores-01.md \"Zero-parse root cause: PL011 baud-change\n# open\").\n#\n# Mechanism: on the RPi5 PL011 UART, a fresh open whose baud differs\n# from the previous open does not always apply the new divisor —\n# sometimes it stays stale (undersampled garbage), sometimes it locks\n# bit-misaligned (full byte count, every CRC fails). So after putting\n# the device on 921600, we make every measured open a baud *change*:\n# a wrong-baud `--baud 115200` open immediately before each captured\n# `--baud 921600` read. ~20% of those opens fail; the device never\n# changes and the next open always parses clean.\n#\n# This script configures the device itself (volatile — a power cycle\n# reverts to the flash 115200) and leaves it on 921600 + a heavy\n# 7-field stream.\n#\n# Usage: repro.sh [OUT_DIR] [N] [START_BAUD]\n#   OUT_DIR    capture directory (default ./tmp)\n#   N          stress iterations (default 20)\n#   START_BAUD device's current baud (default 115200, the flash\n#              default; pass 921600 to re-run on an already-switched\n#              device)\nset -u\nOUT=${1:-./tmp}\nN=${2:-20}\nSTART_BAUD=${3:-115200}\nmkdir -p \"$OUT\"\n\n# 1. Put the device on 921600 with a heavy 7-field binary + YMR\n#    stream. Each step opens at 921600 after the switch, leaving the\n#    host PL011 divisor there.\necho \"configuring device: ${START_BAUD} -> 921600, heavy 7-field + YMR...\"\nrw-vn100 baud 921600 --baud \"$START_BAUD\" || {\n  echo \"baud switch failed — is the device at ${START_BAUD}?\"\n  echo \"(re-run with START_BAUD=921600, or power-cycle the device)\"\n  exit 1\n}\nrw-vn100 set-ascii=off --baud 921600\nrw-vn100 set-bin=off --baud 921600\nrw-vn100 set-bin-fields=time,ypr,quat,gyro,accel,imu,magpres --baud 921600\nrw-vn100 set-bin-hz=200 --baud 921600\nrw-vn100 set-bin=on --baud 921600\nrw-vn100 set-ascii=ymr --baud 921600\n\n# 2. Sanity check: a same-baud 921600 read must parse before we start\n#    the baud-change stress, or the test means nothing.\necho \"sanity check (clean 921600 read):\"\nrw-vn100 bench 1 --baud 921600 || {\n  echo \"device not streaming cleanly at 921600 — aborting\"\n  exit 1\n}\n\n# 3. Stress loop: each captured 921600 read is preceded by a\n#    now-genuinely-wrong-baud 115200 open (device is at 921600), so\n#    every measured open is a baud change.\necho \"stress loop (${N}x baud-change opens):\"\nfor i in $(seq 1 \"$N\"); do\n  rw-vn100 bench 1 --baud 115200 >/dev/null 2>&1   # wrong-baud flip\n  ts=$(date +%H%M%S%3N)\n  printf \"run %2d: \" \"$i\"\n  rw-vn100 bench 1 --baud 921600 --capture \"$OUT/cap-alt-r${i}-${ts}.bin\" 2>&1 \\\n    | grep -E \"ASCII:|Binary:|none|error\" | tr '\\n' ' '\n  echo\ndone\n"
}
```

> AGENT

Script validated end-to-end — it now stands alone:

1. **Configures** the device (`921600 → 921600` no-op switch here, then the full quiet config: off → 7-field mask → 200 Hz → on → YMR).
2. **Sanity check** passed — a clean same-baud 921600 read parsed fully (VNYMR + 110 B/frame 7-field binary, 268 kbit/s; the decode even shows temp 31.3°C / pres 101.4 kPa, device healthy).
3. **Stress loop** ran the baud-change opens (3/3 clean here — ~20% rate means 3 iterations often catches none; the preserved 20-run triplets hold the actual failures).

So the "wrong-baud flip" comment is now true by construction — the script guarantees the device is at 921600 before the loop, and it fails loudly if it can't get there or the sanity read doesn't parse.

The commit file set is unchanged (chores-01, todo.md, .gitignore, `test-data/zero-parse/` with the corrected `repro.sh` + 6 captures). Ready to finalize `0.3.0-5.5` — bump the version, run the cargo cycle, and show the commit body for the push?

I think we should do at least one more test, disconnect/reconnect the vn-100
from the rpi5 so we power cycle and then retest so see it fail again, thoughts?

> AGENT

Agreed — and it's the right instinct to do this *before* committing the write-up, since it's the final check on the repro claims. Reproducing from a genuinely cold device + flash defaults proves the phenomenon isn't an artifact of accumulated session state, and it exercises the script's **default path** (`START_BAUD=115200`) — which we haven't run; the smoke test used `START_BAUD=921600` warm.

One clear-eyed nuance so we know what we're testing: the bug is **host-side** (the rpi5 PL011 divisor), so power-cycling the *VN-100* resets the device, not the host mechanism. `/dev/ttyAMA0` is the rpi5's own UART — disconnecting the VN-100 won't remove the tty, and the PL011 isn't reset. So this validates **"reproduces from the device's flash-default state"** and the **script's full default flow**, not a host cold-boot. That's still well worth having, and there's a bonus: the device comes back at 115200, so the script's first `baud 921600 --baud 115200` switch runs against a real 115200 device for the first time.

Proposed flow once you've reconnected:
1. **Confirm flash defaults** — `rw-vn100 bench 2 --baud 115200` should show VNYMR ASCII ~40 Hz, `Binary: none` (binary reverts to off on power cycle).
2. **Run the repro at defaults** — `bash test-data/zero-parse/repro.sh ./tmp 20` (START_BAUD=115200) and watch for the ~20% failures.

I'll hold `0.3.0-5.5` until we've seen it fail from cold. Power-cycle the VN-100 whenever you're ready, and tell me when it's reconnected — I'll run the confirm + repro (or you can, your call).

> AGENT

**Reproduced from cold — runs 15 and 18 failed (2/20).** So the zero-parse reproduces from a freshly power-cycled VN-100 at flash defaults, through the script's default `START_BAUD=115200` path. It's not an artifact of accumulated session state, and the self-configuring script works from scratch. Validation success.

**One new wrinkle worth flagging:** during the config phase the script hit `Error: device error 0x05 (not enough parameters)` — yet the **sanity check passed** (full 7-field @ 200 Hz + VNYMR, 268 kbit/s), so the device ended up correctly configured and the test ran validly (the script's sanity gate did its job — it would have aborted otherwise).

We think that 0x05 is **the same baud-change-open misalignment, but on the outgoing *command* path**: a misaligned 921600 open garbled a `VNWRG` write, so the device saw a truncated command (too few params), and a later fresh-open command corrected the state. That would extend the bug beyond passive reads to **transacts/writes** — a TX-side manifestation of the same PL011 issue. It's an inference, though; confirming it would mean re-running with the full TX/RX visible to catch which write got mangled.

So where we are:
- The repro is solid and now confirmed from cold (warm 4/20 + cold 2/20).
- The 0x05 is a related, unconfirmed observation that the bug may also corrupt outgoing commands.

Two paths:
1. **Finalize now** — I'd add a one-line "also reproduced from a cold device (2/20)" to the write-up, and a short note that the same misalignment appears to hit writes (the 0x05), then commit `0.3.0-5.5`.
2. **Dig into the 0x05 first** — re-run with full output to confirm the write-path hypothesis before committing.

My rec: option 1 — record the cold confirmation and the write-path observation (marked as a hypothesis), and finalize. The 0x05 is the same root cause, not a new bug, so it doesn't need to block the write-up. Which do you want?

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/nps-gnc/data/prgs/nps-gnc/rw-vn100/test-data/zero-parse/README.md",
  "content": "# zero-parse reproduction\n\nEvidence and a repro script for the passive-`bench` **zero-parse** —\na full byte read off the wire that parses into zero messages/frames\n(`ASCII: none / Binary: none`). Root-cause analysis:\n[chores-01](../../notes/chores/chores-01.md) \"Zero-parse root cause:\nPL011 baud-change open\". Tracked as Todo #1.\n\nWe think the RPi5 PL011 UART does not reliably apply the new baud\ndivisor on a fresh open when the open baud differs from the previous\nopen — sometimes it stays stale (undersampled), sometimes it locks\nbit-misaligned (full byte count, every CRC fails).\n\n## `repro.sh`\n\nSelf-configuring: puts the device on 921600 + a heavy 7-field binary\n+ YMR stream, sanity-checks a clean read, then alternates a\nwrong-baud `--baud 115200` open before each `--baud 921600` read, so\nevery measured open is a baud *change*. ~20% fail. See its header\nfor usage and device prep.\n\n## Captures\n\nRaw `bench --capture` dumps — the bytes the scanner saw. Diff a\n`*-fail` against its `*-before` / `*-after` (or the clean\n`../both-streams.bin`, same 7-field + YMR config).\n\n- `misframe-{before,fail,after}.bin` — full-misframe mode: the\n  `-fail` is ~26.9 KB, frame-shaped but every CRC fails (full\n  ~269 kbit/s, none parsed); before/after parse clean.\n- `stale-{before,fail,after}.bin` — stale-divisor mode: the `-fail`\n  is ~2.2 KB, undersampled garbage (~24 kbit/s); before/after clean.\n- `cold-misframe-{1,2}.bin` — the two failures from the cold-start\n  reproduction below.\n\n## Reproductions\n\n- **Warm** (continuously-powered device), `repro.sh` alternating\n  `--baud`: 4/20 failed — 2 misframe, 2 stale (the triplets above).\n- **Cold** (VN-100 power-cycled to flash defaults), `repro.sh`\n  defaults (`START_BAUD=115200`): 2/20 failed (both full-misframe,\n  `cold-misframe-{1,2}.bin`). Confirms it is not session state. A\n  config-phase `$VNERR 0x05` (not enough parameters) also appeared;\n  we think the same misalignment can garble an *outgoing* command\n  (TX side), not just passive reads — unconfirmed.\n"
}
```

```
Reproduced and root-caused Todo #1's intermittent zero-parse — a
full byte read off the wire that parses into zero messages/frames.
The RPi5 PL011 doesn't reliably apply the new baud divisor on a
fresh open when the open baud differs from the previous open. Host-
side, not the device — a re-open clears it. Two modes: stale-divisor
undersample, and bit-misaligned full count. Reproduced 4/20 warm and
2/20 from a cold power-cycle.

- notes/chores/chores-01.md: new "Zero-parse root cause: PL011
  baud-change open" subsection — define the term, the recipe and
  4/20 rate, the two modes with byte evidence, host-side
  conclusion, cold re-confirmation, and fix directions
- notes/todo.md: re-scope Todo #1 from investigate to fix
- test-data/zero-parse/: clean→fail→clean triplets per mode, the
  two cold-start failures, repro.sh (self-configuring), and README
- .gitignore: ignore /tmp (capture scratch)
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: version-of-record 0.3.0-5.4 -> 0.3.0-5.5
```

> AGENT

This is a sharp catch, and it overturns part of my characterization. You're right — `0d 0a fa 01 39 07` recurring means the **framing is intact**: byte boundaries are correct, CRLF line-ends and `0xFA` sync + `01 39 07` header all land in place. That is *not* "shifted garbage," which is how I (and the original note) described the misframe case. Let me decode the corruption pattern, because it looks specific.

Eyeballing that ASCII tail (`...,k05v.7t2,k07t.3r6,k086.460,-00.1781,...`): a clean VNYMR field is digits/`+`/`-`/`.`/`,`, but here some chars became lowercase letters — and every one is exactly the right char with **bit 6 (0x40) set**: `+`(2B)→`k`(6B), `0`(30)→`p`(70), `.`(2E)→`n`(6E), `,`(2C)→`l`(6C), `*`(2A)→`j`(6A), `6`(36)→`v`(76)… all `^0x40`. Let me verify that holds across the whole file:

The script had two bugs (a heredoc-escaping issue on the clean CRLF count, and it anchored on intact `$VN` — which the fail file doesn't have, since the `$` itself gets corrupted, hence `$VN: fail=0`). But the real signal is already there: **130/200 frame headers and 42 CRLFs survive intact** in the corrupted file. And your clean `misframe-before.bin` dump shows a *perfect* VNYMR line + frame — so before=clean, fail=bit-6-peppered. > AGENT

Rigorously confirmed now — and it's a real correction to what I wrote:

- **All 200 frames are physically present.** Strict `fa 01 39 07` matched 130; allowing a **bit-6 flip on any header byte** recovers **201** (all of them). So no bytes are lost or shifted — every frame is there, just peppered.
- **+1674 lowercase bytes** vs clean (3104 vs 1430) — those are ASCII digits/punctuation with bit 6 flipped to letters.
- Your clean `misframe-before.bin` dump confirms the baseline: a perfect `$VNYMR,...*6C\r\n` + frame.

So the mechanism is **not** "bit-misaligned / shifted garbage" (my wording, and the original note's) — that's wrong. It's **bit 6 (D6) intermittently flipping**: set on `0x2X/0x3X` data (`+`→`k`, `0`→`p`, digit→lowercase), and *cleared* on `0xFA` (→`0xBA`), which is why 70 headers missed the strict match. XOR `0x40` back and the data is valid. The byte clock is correct (framing intact); a single data bit is just wrong intermittently, and over ~110-byte frames every CRC/checksum trips → 0 parsed.

Two consequences:

1. **The just-pushed write-up is partly wrong** and needs a correction commit. The misframe mode isn't a misaligned/unapplied divisor.
2. **The two modes are more distinct than I claimed.** *Low/stale* = the divisor genuinely didn't apply (gross 8× error, framing broken, few bytes). *Misframe* = the divisor **did** apply (framing perfect, full byte count) but **D6 flips**. So "the PL011 doesn't apply the new divisor" explains *low*, not *misframe*.

And the striking open question: **why specifically D6?** A baud/clock error would smear across adjacent bits and break framing — but this is one fixed bit position, framing intact. That points *below* the byte layer — RX sampling / signal integrity at 921600 on a marginal open — not a divisor value. I genuinely don't have the "why" yet; we think it's hardware/sampling-level, host-side and below the application (the tool doesn't touch the bytes).

The fix is unaffected — reopen-on-bad-open detects both modes (CRC fails either way).

I'd like to **correct the write-up** (reframe misframe as "framing intact, sporadic D6 bit-flips," split the two mechanisms, flag the D6 mystery). Do that now as a follow-up commit, or do you want to dig into the D6 "why" first — e.g., capture more failures to see if it's always D6, or test a lower baud (460800) to see if the bit position or rate changes?

We think the RPi5 PL011\ndoes not reliably apply the new baud divisor on a fresh open when\nthe requested baud differs from the previous open's — sometimes\nleaving it stale (low), sometimes locking it bit-misaligned\n(full-misframe). This is why the old configure/measure bench was\nimmune: its pre-measure `transact_retry` was a write→read that\nforced the divisor to settle (or failed loudly). The passive bench\nreads cold.",
```json
  "new_string": "It is host-side, not the device: the stream is continuous, only\nsome opens fail, and a re-open clears it. The two modes differ,\nthough. The **low** case is a genuine divisor failure — the open\nkeeps the stale 115200 divisor, an 8× error that breaks framing.\nThe **misframe** case is not — framing is intact and the byte clock\nis right, only B6 flips. We think both stem from a marginal\nhigh-baud open (the low case a wholly-unapplied divisor, the\nmisframe case a sampling / signal-integrity glitch below the byte\nlayer), but the **B6 specificity is unexplained** — a baud/clock\nerror would smear across adjacent bits and break framing, which it\ndoes not. This is why the old configure/measure bench was immune:\nits pre-measure `transact_retry` was a write→read that forced a\nclean lock (or failed loudly). The passive bench reads cold."
}
```

```
9	We think the RPi5 PL011 UART does not reliably apply the new baud
10	divisor on a fresh open when the open baud differs from the previous
11	open — sometimes it stays stale (undersampled), sometimes it locks
12	bit-misaligned (full byte count, every CRC fails).
13	
14	## `repro.sh`
15	
16	Self-configuring: puts the device on 921600 + a heavy 7-field binary
17	+ YMR stream, sanity-checks a clean read, then alternates a
18	wrong-baud `--baud 115200` open before each `--baud 921600` read, so
19	every measured open is a baud *change*. ~20% fail. See its header
20	for usage and device prep.
21	
22	## Captures
23	
24	Raw `bench --capture` dumps — the bytes the scanner saw. Diff a
25	`*-fail` against its `*-before` / `*-after` (or the clean
26	`../both-streams.bin`, same 7-field + YMR config).
27	
28	- `misframe-{before,fail,after}.bin` — full-misframe mode: the
29	  `-fail` is ~26.9 KB, frame-shaped but every CRC fails (full
30	  ~269 kbit/s, none parsed); before/after parse clean.
31	- `stale-{before,fail,after}.bin` — stale-divisor mode: the `-fail`
32	  is ~2.2 KB, undersampled garbage (~24 kbit/s); before/after clean.
33	- `cold-misframe-{1,2}.bin` — the two failures from the cold-start
34	  reproduction below.
35	
36	## Reproductions
37	
38	- **Warm** (continuously-powered device), `repro.sh` alternating
```

```
355	### Zero-parse root cause: PL011 baud-change open (confirmed 2026-06-28)
356	
357	Both modes above reproduced on the rpi5/TTL using the `--capture`
358	flag (the AM `RWVN100_DUMP` idea, landed as `bench --capture
359	<path>`). The recipe makes every measured open a baud *change*:
360	alternate a `bench --baud 115200` (wrong baud — device at 921600)
361	immediately before each `bench --baud 921600`. 4 of 20 such opens
362	failed (20%); the streaming device never changed, and the next
363	open always parsed clean.
364	
365	- **Full-misframe** (`test-data/zero-parse/misframe-fail.bin`) —
366	  "misframe" is a misnomer: framing is **intact**. All ~200 frames
367	  are present (tolerating a bit-6 flip on the header bytes recovers
368	  201 matches vs 130 for a strict `fa 01 39 07`), and CRLFs survive.
369	  The byte clock is right — instead, **bit 6 (B6)** flips
370	  intermittently. A byte's bits are numbered 0..7 LSB-first, so B6
371	  is the seventh. B6 is set on `0x2X/0x3X` data (`+`→`k`, digit→
372	  lowercase) and cleared on `0xFA`→`0xBA`, so XOR `0x40` recovers
373	  valid bytes, but over a ~110 B frame every CRC / checksum trips
374	  and 0 parse. The "full ~269 kbit/s, none parsed" case. Not a
375	  shifted/misaligned window (an earlier guess) — the divisor applied
376	  correctly.
377	- **Low / stale divisor** (`test-data/zero-parse/stale-fail.bin`) —
378	  ~2.2 KB read, no structure (1 `0xFA`, 3 `$` in the file): the
379	  921600 open kept the stale 115200 divisor and undersampled the
380	  stream 8×. The "~24 kbit/s after a wrong-baud open" case.
381	
382	`test-data/zero-parse/` holds each mode as a clean→fail→clean
383	triplet (`*-before` / `*-fail` / `*-after`, every captured open
384	preceded by a 115200 flip) plus `repro.sh`, the script that
385	demonstrates it.
386	
387	It is host-side, not the device: the stream is continuous, only
388	some opens fail, and a re-open clears it. The two modes differ,
389	though. The **low** case is a genuine divisor failure — the open
390	keeps the stale 115200 divisor, an 8× error that breaks framing.
391	The **misframe** case is not — framing is intact and the byte clock
392	is right, only B6 flips. We think both stem from a marginal
393	high-baud open (the low case a wholly-unapplied divisor, the
394	misframe case a sampling / signal-integrity glitch below the byte
395	layer), but the **B6 specificity is unexplained** — a baud/clock
396	error would smear across adjacent bits and break framing, which it
397	does not. This is why the old configure/measure bench was immune:
398	its pre-measure `transact_retry` was a write→read that forced a
399	clean lock (or failed loudly). The passive bench reads cold.
400	
401	Re-confirmed from cold: with the VN-100 power-cycled to flash
402	defaults, `repro.sh` (default `START_BAUD=115200`) failed 2/20 (both
403	B6-flip), so the symptom is not an artifact of session state. A
404	config-phase `$VNERR 0x05` (not enough parameters) appeared on that
405	run too — we think the same corruption can also garble an *outgoing*
406	command (TX side), not just passive reads, though that is
407	unconfirmed.
408	
409	Fix direction (the remaining Todo):
```

> DEVELOPER

Clarify the reopen-on-bad-open bug

> AGENT

Good catch — "reopen-on-bad-open" as written is imprecise in a way that matters. Let me clarify it, because there's a real subtlety the current note glosses over.

**What it's meant to be:** `measure` checks an early slice of the window; if nothing parses (0 valid frames/lines), the open was bad, so close the port, reopen, and measure again — because reopening re-runs the baud setup (`tcsetattr`), which is the thing that recovers.

**The imprecision (the "bug"):** the note says *"reopens the port, **since a re-open clears it**"* — implying **one** reopen fixes it. It doesn't, reliably. Each open has its own failure probability, so the fix has to **reopen-and-retry until a clean read (bounded)**, not reopen once. Our own data says so:

- **Same-baud opens are clean** — 40/40 in the loops. So once the port is correctly at the target baud, a reopen at that same baud is reliable.
- **Baud-*change* opens are ~20% bad** — the 4/20.

And that splits by failure mode:
- **B6-flip case:** the divisor is already at 921600 (framing was intact), so the reopen is a *same-baud* open → reliably clean. One reopen ≈ fine.
- **Stale-divisor case:** the port is stuck at 115200, so reopening *to* 921600 is itself a *baud-change* open → ~20% chance of landing bad again. So a single reopen can fail; it must retry.

So the accurate statement is: **detect 0-parse early → reopen and re-measure, looping until it parses, capped at N tries** (and on exhausting N, surface a clear error rather than silently returning none). The "a re-open clears it" phrasing undersells that the reopen is itself probabilistic — especially in the stale case, where the reopen repeats the risky baud-change.

Want me to fold this into the notes — rewrite the chores `Reopen-on-bad-open` bullet and Todo #1's fix line as "reopen-and-retry until a clean read (bounded N), error if exhausted," with the per-mode nuance? That'd be a small follow-up commit.

- **Issue #1** — RS-232 rig: a high-baud fresh-process open makes the *device* wedge (goes silent at every baud, needs a power cycle).
- **The zero-parse** — TTL/PL011: a high-baud *baud-change* open makes the *host* misread (B6-flip or stale-divisor), device keeps streaming fine, a reopen recovers.

Same family (high-baud fresh opens on the rpi5), different failure (device wedge vs host read corruption), different link (RS-232 vs TTL) — so a distinct Issue #3 that cross-references #1 fits cleanly. > DEVELOPER

Add Issue #3 to bugs.md, is this a work-around to the zero-parse "issue" we just analyzed?

> AGENT

Yes — reopen-and-retry (and the termios re-assert) are **work-arounds, not fixes**. The root cause is the RPi5 PL011 mis-applying the baud divisor on a baud-change open, which lives in the host UART driver/hardware, below the tool. We can't make the PL011 open cleanly; we can only detect a bad open and retry until we get a good one. So the tool papers over it. I'll frame Issue #3's mitigation exactly that way.

Anchor matches the github-slugger output, validation green, `0.3.0-5.7`. And to answer your question plainly: **yes — reopen-and-retry is a work-around, not a fix.** The PL011 mis-applies the divisor at the host UART level, below the tool; we can only detect the bad open and retry until we get a good one. Issue #3 says exactly that.

> DEVELOPER

what does "a stable bugs.md  Issue" mean?

> AGENT

"Stable" there means the Issue gets a **permanent ID that never changes** — that's bugs.md's defining property, and it's the whole reason to register the bug there rather than leave it only in Todo #1.

From bugs.md's own header: *"The Issue # is a permanent, monotonic id — assigned once from the allocator below, never reused or renumbered — so the heading doubles as a stable anchor (`#issue-N-…`) that todo / chores / commits can link to."*

The contrast is with **Todo numbers**, which are *not* stable — they're positional priority ranks, and `vc-x1 fix-todo` renumbers them, so "Todo #1" today could be "Todo #4" next week. You can't durably link to a Todo by number. A bugs.md Issue # (and its `#issue-3-…` heading anchor) stays put forever, so chores, the Todo, and future commits can all point at "Bug #3" and it always resolves.

So "register the confirmed zero-parse as a stable bugs.md Issue" = give it a permanent #3 that won't shift, as the durable anchor for everything that references it.

If "stable" reads as vague jargon, I can reword that commit-body line to something plainer, e.g.:

> *Register the confirmed zero-parse as a bugs.md Issue, giving it a permanent ID (Bug #3) as the durable cross-reference anchor — a sibling to Issue #1 …*

Want me to use that phrasing instead, then push?

> AGENT

We're at a clean point — Bug #3 fully recorded (registry, Todo, analysis, evidence). The natural continuation is **implementing the fix**: the reopen-and-retry work-around for Todo #1 / Bug #3. (The other two open threads — "why B6" and the full host-cold test — are a likely rabbit-hole and a you-power-cycle-the-rpi5 task, respectively.)

Before I touch the core read path, here's the design I'd implement — it's small but has a few real decisions:

**Where.** The retry has to wrap the *port open*, which lives in `run()` (lib.rs) — `bench` only gets a `&mut S: Read` and can't reopen it. So I'd put a bench-specific open→probe→reopen loop in `run()` (the open currently happens once before the command match; bench would get its own retrying open).

**Detect (kept passive).** A "bad open" = **0 ASCII messages and 0 binary frames in a short probe window** (~0.25 s). At our 40 Hz+ streams a clean open yields plenty in 0.25 s, so 0 is a reliable bad-open signal — and it's read-only, so `bench` stays passive (no priming write, which the chores note floated but which would break passivity).

**Retry.** Bad probe → drop the port, reopen at the same config, probe again, bounded **N = 5**. All N bad → the existing "saw no ASCII/binary" error, now meaning "couldn't get a clean open in N tries." Once a probe is clean, run the full `secs` measure on that same good fd (no further reopen).

**Scope.** Just `bench` for now. The `set-*`/`get-*` transacts have `transact_retry`, but note it only re-sends the command, it doesn't reopen — so a stale-divisor open would still defeat them. I'd flag extending reopen-to-transacts as a follow-up, not do it here.

Land as `feat: 0.3.0-5.8`.

Two things to confirm before I code: the **probe window (0.25 s)** and **N = 5** — good defaults, or different? And: implement now, or would you rather pin down "why B6" first?
